Agent Distraction

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

What is easy for humans to skim past in code, agents see as a call to action. They do not know better, latching on to that which is littered across the files read during the planning and research phase of the coding journey.

As agents' agency through tools and skills grows the ability for side-quests scales proportionally. Obsolete specs, long resolved bugs, and lagging documentation no longer rest in long forgotten corners of the codebase, but rather are unintentionally ingested as context indirecting and misdirecting the next wave of coders - the agents. This indirection is not free: in the best case it only costs tokens, steps, and time - in the worst case it distracts and grounds the agent on falsehoods leading to incorrect logic and decisions during implementation.

Measuring how often agents fall into these traps across millions of monthly sessions is impossible with manual human review - we need an objective signal derivable from the code itself. Inline TODO(bug/...) comments provide the perfect proxy. TODOs tend to be left behind long after the underlying bug is resolved or abandoned, while the linked bug ticket acts as the canonical record. By cross-referencing every TODO an agent encounters against the TODO's bug status and the user's prompt, we can automatically separate valid work from wasted detours at scale.

By leveraging the distributed traces of agent interactions across thousands of engineers and over hundreds of millions of individual agent steps, we quantify the impact of TODO distractions in the codebase and how agents act on them:

Controlling for developer, codebase complexity, task type, model tier, number of files read, initial prompt length, and the step where the bug reference appeared, using a multi-way fixed-effects regression with developer-clustered standard errors, we see that unprompted bug detours add substantial overhead (all stat sig p<0.0001). Per session:

The Obsolete Detour: In the following trajectory we can observe how this drift plays out. During an investigation into an unrelated bug The agent views SurfaceControlTest.java and sees // TODO(bug/XXX)

This bug, while fixed in 2023, remains lingering in the codebase leading to subsequent investigation distraction across Steps with the agent digging into the bug to learn more.

The Eager Agent: In the trajectory the agent encountered a TODO in the bug it was investigating and decided (unprompted) to "fix" the code

The agent executed multiple Code Search and internal knowledge search queries for the bug ultimately resulting in the agent changing the ModelDownloaderV1 code. Later in the trajectory the human challenged the scope creep.

With the model when admitting it was an incorrect increase in scope:

While in both of these cases they were only trivial to minor detours - they were real distractions costing time, tokens, and human intervention.

As part of addressing this finding at scale we created agents that scrubbed the totality of our codebases, finding bug references in the code that were marked as fixed or obsolete for cleanup. That step alone however was not enough to determine if the TODOs in the codebase were load-bearing or not. The agents had to go further looking at the history of the bug, the file, and changes to the codebase, at times spanning years of history. Only in cases where the agent was able to confirm that the TODO and the intent behind the TODO was obsolete did our agent send a change for review to delete it from the codebase. As a result of this multi-layer approach, our todo cleaner has been able to remove thousands of actually obsolete references over the last 6 months exceeding 95% acceptance from human reviewers.

In recap, as we task agents on more and more ambitious problems, "agent friendly codebases" require more than just the code in the hotpath being healthy. The forgotten corners of the codebase now more than ever carry a significant tax levied against agents.

Tooling like presubmit analyzers, entire corpus scanners, and automated coding agents to remediate dead-todos offer out of the box ready to scale solutions to address the tax enabling a healthier and distraction free codebase for agents to thrive.

Projects that once were on the backburner to cleanup lingering debt but deprioritized due to perceived low value need to be reevaluated with the new agents acting at scale on your codebase.

I Gave My Accounts-Payable Agent Hindsight Memory. It Stopped Asking.

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Every month, an invoice from a steel distributor arrives priced 3% above the purchase order, and every month someone in accounts payable stops, opens the contract, finds clause 7.2 (monthly steel-index adjustment, capped at 5%), and approves it. The matching software flags it perfectly. It just has no idea that this exact question was answered last month, and the month before.

That is the gap I built Ledgerwise for: an agent that sits after the 3-way match and resolves invoice exceptions the way your desk resolved them last time — and knows when that history isn't enough.

An AP desk matches three documents: the purchase order (what we agreed to buy), the goods receipt (what actually arrived), and the invoice (what the vendor wants paid). When they disagree you get an exception: a price variance, a short shipment, a GST rate that doesn't match the contract, a surcharge line nobody ordered, a re-sent duplicate, or a vendor letter saying "we've changed our bank account."

Detecting those is a solved problem, and in Ledgerwise it's deliberately boring: matching.py is deterministic Python with no model and no memory. It finds exceptions and measures them — a percentage for price, rupees for a surcharge, units for a short shipment.

The interesting part is deciding. That's agent.py, and it runs one loop per exception:

The memory layer is Hindsight, an open-source agent memory system from Vectorize. I picked it over rolling my own vector store because AP history isn't a pile of documents to search — it's a set of decisions with actors, amounts and dates, and I wanted something that extracts and organises those facts rather than just embedding paragraphs.

The obvious design is: recall similar past decisions, and if the top result says APPROVE, approve. Run that against the replay data and it looks great for a few weeks — until a logistics vendor sends a ₹6,800 fuel surcharge. Every earlier surcharge from that vendor was approved, but they were ₹1,200–₹2,400, and the agreed cap is ₹2,500. Similarity says "same vendor, same exception, approved." The money says otherwise.

That case reshaped the whole design. The question stopped being "what does memory say?" and became "is memory enough here?"

Each human decision is retained as one string with two parts: a prose sentence that Hindsight's retain pipeline extracts facts and entities from, and a structured line my policy can parse back out.

document_id keeps a re-decision on the same invoice from becoming a duplicate memory, and timestamp uses the invoice date rather than "now", so temporal questions work.

The magnitude matters as much as the decision. "Approved a 2.8% variance" and "approved a 4.2% variance" are different precedents.

Before touching an exception, the agent asks a question in plain English:

include_chunks=True was the detail that made this work. Recall returns the extracted facts, which are great for humans and for reflection, but the policy needs exact numbers. Asking for source chunks brings the structured precedent line back alongside each fact.

With precedents in hand, the rules are short and intentionally conservative:

The LLM (Groq, openai/gpt-oss-120b) writes the explanation and the vendor email. It never makes the pay/no-pay decision, and if it's down, a template takes over.

I replay eight weeks of invoices from six vendors, each with one realistic recurring quirk, and play the AP lead whenever the agent escalates.

That's the share of exception invoices resolved with no human, on the replay dataset. Across the run, zero of the automatic decisions were ones the AP lead would have overturned.

Week 1, Kaveri invoices 18% GST on a 12% line. No memory, so it comes to me. I reject and type: "HSN 2839 is contracted at 12% GST; ask Kaveri for a credit note. Never pay the 18% version."

Week 3, same quirk. Recall returns two human rejections. The agent rejects on its own, cites both invoice numbers, and drafts the credit-note email. Nobody touches it.

Week 5, steel at +3.1%. Humans had approved up to +2.8%. The agent escalates with "leaning approve" instead of extrapolating. I approve, and the learned range widens.

Week 6, Meridian Electricals sends a "we've moved banks" letter. Memory has six clean Meridian invoices. It escalates anyway.

The "Brief" button calls Hindsight's reflect for a vendor and returns what a new joiner should know before the payment run: fuel surcharges up to ₹2,500 are pre-agreed, one at ₹6,800 was rejected. That's the institutional knowledge that usually leaves when the AP lead goes on leave.

Youtube link: https://youtu.be/3vHxH_v8v6

Try the live demo: https://ledgerwise-1ydb.onrender.com/

1. The reason field is the product. I made the UI refuse to save a decision without a typed reason. Those sentences are what escalations show, what reflect summarises, and what makes the memory auditable. "Approved" is a log line; "approved under clause 7.2, index sheet attached" is knowledge.

2. Store the magnitude, not just the outcome. Similarity search will happily match "same vendor, same exception" and ignore that this one is three times bigger. Every precedent carries its number, and the policy compares numbers.

3. Decide what memory is allowed to override before you write any recall code. Bank changes, unknown POs and anything outside a learned range are human-only by design. Writing those guardrails first made the rest of the policy much easier to reason about.

4. Don't let the agent cite itself. Retaining the agent's own decisions is useful for audit and reflection. Counting them as precedent is how a system drifts.

5. Make memory configurable, not magical. A skeptical, literal bank disposition (skepticism 5, literalism 4) fit an AP desk better than the defaults. One approval of a 3% variance should not become permission for "price variances" in general.

The honest limitation: Ledgerwise is only as good as the reasons people type. A desk that clicks "approve" with "ok" gets an agent that can count but can't explain.

If you want to try the memory layer yourself, the Hindsight docs get you to a working retain/recall loop quickly, and Vectorize's explainer on what agent memory is is a good read on why this is different from RAG. Ledgerwise's code is on GitHub: https://github.com/SomeswararaoTellakula/ledgerwise

Shout-out to Code.in and the Hindsight team at Vectorize.

AI Agents Are Changing Software Engineering: 4 Major IT Developments This Week

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

November 8, 2026 | AI, Software Engineering, DevOps, Cybersecurity

For years, developers have been told that artificial intelligence would transform software development.

In November 2026, we're seeing something more interesting than another AI model release.

The infrastructure, workflows, and security practices around software engineering are changing to accommodate AI agents.

We're moving beyond autocomplete and chat-based coding assistants toward systems that can plan tasks, modify codebases, run tests, and coordinate multiple development activities.

Four major developments this week highlight where the IT industry is heading.

And for developers, engineering teams, and technology leaders, they raise an important question:

Are our software development processes ready for AI agents?

On November 4, 2026, Meta's acquisition of Conductor, an AI coding startup, became public.

Conductor enables developers to coordinate multiple AI coding agents working across parallel workspaces.

Instead of assigning every task to one coding assistant, developers can organize work across multiple agents.

Imagine building a web application with separate agents handling:

The potential advantage isn't simply generating code faster.

It's coordinating independent development tasks.

For development teams, agent orchestration could eventually become another layer in the engineering toolchain, similar to CI/CD platforms and cloud infrastructure.

However, parallel agents also introduce new challenges:

Running five agents doesn't automatically make a team five times more productive.

The real engineering challenge is coordinating their work without sacrificing reliability.

Source: TechCrunch — Meta acquires AI coding startup Conductor

On November 5, GitHub published details of its new Git storage architecture.

One of the most interesting findings came from its internal testing.

These are internal test results, not a claim that ordinary repositories currently experience this traffic.

GitHub is designing infrastructure for a future where automated agents produce significantly more repository activity.

Traditional software development generally assumes that humans perform most repository operations.

Developers create branches, make commits, open pull requests, and merge approved changes.

AI agents can accelerate these activities and increase their frequency.

That introduces new infrastructure requirements:

This doesn't mean developers should maximize commits or pull requests.

In fact, more changes can create more review overhead.

Developers may spend less time typing routine implementations and more time on system architecture, specifications, integration, and verification.

GitHub's November 6 article on agentic engineering reinforces this distinction: faster code generation doesn't automatically solve the work surrounding software delivery.

Perhaps the most urgent development this week came from Microsoft Threat Intelligence.

On November 5, Microsoft published research describing threat actors using agentic AI tooling to identify exposed credentials in public GitHub repositories and abuse compromised cloud access.

In one observed incident, an AI agent progressed from identifying exposed secrets to extracting data from Azure Key Vault in approximately five minutes.

This isn't evidence that every AI agent is dangerous.

It demonstrates how automation can accelerate existing cybersecurity threats.

Exposing an API key in a repository has always been a security problem.

But when automated tools can discover and exploit exposed credentials more quickly, the window for defenders to respond becomes smaller.

The same security fundamentals remain essential:

Deleting an exposed credential from a repository does not make that credential safe again.

If a coding agent can access a production database, deployment environment, or privileged cloud account, its permissions become part of the system's security boundary.

A safer approach is to isolate agent execution and limit access to only what a task requires.

Agent-generated code should pass through the same security and deployment controls as human-written code.

Source: Microsoft Security — Threat actors use agentic AI tooling to find and abuse exposed secrets

On November 4, Thoughtworks published its analysis of macro technology trends for November 2026.

One of its central themes was the challenge of moving AI agents from small experiments into organization-wide software development.

A developer experimenting with a coding agent is very different from a company coordinating agents across dozens of engineering teams.

At scale, several questions become critical.

Context management: How do agents understand architecture decisions, coding standards, business rules, and acceptance criteria?

Cost management: How can teams measure the cost of successfully completed tasks rather than focusing only on token consumption?

Code review: Who verifies the growing volume of AI-generated changes?

Security: What systems can agents access, and who controls those permissions?

Platform ownership: Who maintains the shared infrastructure that agents depend on?

Thoughtworks describes the importance of an agent harness: the surrounding system that provides context, model access, evaluation, sandboxing, identity, cost control, and observability.

It suggests that platform engineering and DevOps practices may become even more important as agentic software development expands.

AI coding tools are becoming easier to access.

But operating them reliably across an engineering organization remains difficult.

The competitive advantage may increasingly come from how well teams integrate AI into their existing development practices—not simply which model they select.

Source: Thoughtworks — Macro trends in the tech industry, November 2026

You don't need an enterprise AI platform or an expensive multi-agent setup to start improving your workflow.

A practical approach is to introduce AI assistance into one controlled development process.

Create an issue with requirements, acceptance criteria, and constraints.

Allow the agent to work on a feature branch rather than directly modifying the production branch.

Validate application behavior, not just whether the generated code compiles.

Check security, architecture, maintainability, and actual business requirements.

Step 5: Deploy through an existing release process.

Keep automated testing, staging environments, and production approvals in place.

This workflow is not limited to AI-generated code. It's good engineering discipline that becomes especially valuable as automation increases.

Rather than measuring how many lines of code an AI agent produces, consider tracking:

These metrics provide a more meaningful picture of engineering productivity than code volume alone.

I don't think that is the most useful question.

AI agents are changing the work developers perform, but producing code is only one part of building software.

Someone still needs to understand the problem, select appropriate architectural trade-offs, protect user data, verify behavior, and take responsibility for the final system.

It doesn't automatically make requirements correct, architecture scalable, or software secure.

The skills that matter will continue to evolve.

I expect developers who combine strong engineering fundamentals with AI-assisted workflows to have more opportunities to build and maintain complex systems.

That includes understanding APIs, databases, testing, security, cloud infrastructure, and software architecture.

This week's developments point toward a broader change in the IT industry.

Meta's Conductor acquisition shows growing commercial interest in coordinating multiple coding agents.

GitHub's infrastructure work reveals the scale that agent-driven development could create.

Microsoft's security research demonstrates why automated development and cybersecurity must evolve together.

Thoughtworks highlights the organizational challenge of turning individual AI experiments into sustainable engineering practices.

The future of software development isn't just about generating code faster. It's about building trustworthy systems that can coordinate human and AI contributions.

For developers, the opportunity is substantial.

But reliability, security, and engineering judgment will matter more—not less—as automation becomes more capable.

How are you using AI agents in your development workflow today?

Are they mostly assisting with individual coding tasks, or have you started experimenting with multiple agents working together?

And what has been your biggest challenge: code quality, security, cost, or reviewing AI-generated changes?

I'd be interested to hear how other developers are approaching this transition.

This article reflects developments reported through November 8, 2026. The practical recommendations and forward-looking views are the author's analysis, not confirmed industry outcomes.

I built a local-first bookmark manager that actually remembers what you saved

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

I saved links faster than I could find them again, so I built Linkit, a free desktop bookmark manager that fetches and summarizes every link as you save it.

A browser bookmark stores a URL and nothing else. That leaves three gaps: keyword search only matches exact titles, you have to open a link to know what it says, and the same article saved from three URLs looks like three entries. Linkit works on the saved content instead.

Summaries and key takeaways. Every saved link is fetched, summarized, and broken into a few takeaways. You scan the summary instead of opening twelve tabs. Point it at any OpenAI-compatible API, or run a local model through Ollama.

Search that matches on meaning. Keyword search fails the moment you don't remember the wording. "How to make large file transfer faster" should find a link whose title says "transfer optimization". Search here embeds your library and matches on vector similarity.

A knowledge graph. Your library renders as an interactive network, connected by shared tags, collections, and semantic similarity. It shows which topics you keep circling and which areas you've never touched.

AI-curated collections. Give it a goal in plain language, like "collect modern frontend tooling and design resources", and it builds a collection out of bookmarks you already saved, writes the title and description, and recommends tags. Inside a collection, a banner keeps suggesting related bookmarks, and every suggestion is an editable preview until you confirm it.

Duplicate detection. Exact URL matches, plus AI-detected near-duplicates where the same content lives at different URLs. Review and merge with one click.

Link health checks. A background scan finds 404s and silently changed pages, so dead links don't quietly rot.

Curation insights. Linkit summarizes your habits on a recurring schedule: the tags you lean on, how far you got through your unread pile, and the highlights worth a second look.

Interface scaling that actually tracks your display. Linkit now has eight interface sizes, and the zoom couples to the system display scale instead of fighting it, clamped to a safe range so the layout never breaks. The layered zoom architecture also eliminated the black borders and stray outer scrollbars that used to show up at non-native scale factors.

Storage mode switching with a confirmation step. Moving between local and cloud storage now opens a dialog that shows a summary of both sides before you commit, and the settings screen has a real data-management section instead of a scattering of buttons.

Import and export you can actually roll back. The import/export utilities now write a portable library document, and if an import goes wrong the app restores from a snapshot rather than leaving you half-migrated. Batch browser import also resolves save conflicts correctly, which stops a large import from colliding with itself.

Simpler AI tag selection and reanalyze. The bookmark analyzer returns at most three suggested tags, and when it has nothing useful it says so instead of showing an empty picker. There's a dedicated reanalyze dialog for re-running analysis on an existing bookmark, and the new-bookmark dialog was extracted into its own component so both entry paths behave the same.

Friendlier sync errors. Sync failures now surface in a dedicated toast with an explicit "return to sign in" action when the cloud session has expired, and an AI authorization dialog no longer blocks saving unrelated sections.

Shortcuts you can actually rebind. The shortcut panel used to accept a single modifier plus one key, so anything else got rejected. Ctrl, Alt, Shift, and Cmd now combine freely, and the panel shows a live preview of the combination as you hold it, with a note when another app already grabbed that combination. The macOS, Linux, and Windows modifier mappings were wrong for everything except plain Cmd-or-Ctrl, which meant Alt and Cmd combinations never reached the app at all on those platforms. The AI tag suggestions in the new-bookmark dialog are buttons now too, so a suggested tag can be picked or created with one click, and the sync indicator stopped flashing a progress bar across the top of the window.

Batch actions when you have a pile to clean. Switch the toolbar into selection mode and the bulk bar appears. A Select all button takes every bookmark in the current view and turns into Deselect all once they are all picked, Shift-click extends a range, and Cmd/Ctrl-click adds single items. From there you can move the selection to another category, delete it, pull it out of a collection, or turn it into a new collection.

A couple of smaller fixes. Writing to a library whose root path is not a directory now returns a stable error code instead of a raw system error, and the zod dependency moved from 4.0.1 to 4.6.7 to close an audit-reported vulnerability.

All bookmarks live in a local SQLite database on your machine. The app runs fully offline and works without an account. If you want multi-device sync, there's an optional Supabase backend with row-level security where every record is bound to your own auth.uid(), unreadable to the developer. That sync is free as well.

Go for the backend, Wails to ship it as a native desktop app, React for the UI. The AI layer is provider-agnostic, so OpenAI, DeepSeek, a local Ollama instance, or anything that speaks the OpenAI API shape will work. Cross-platform binaries ship through GitHub Releases and Homebrew.

Windows, macOS DMG, and Linux AppImage/DEB are on the releases page.

MIT licensed and 100% free. Import from Chrome, Firefox, Edge, or Safari in one click to start from the collection you already have.

If you've ever lost a link you swore you'd come back to, try it: https://github.com/blue-idea/linkit

If it helps, a star genuinely helps other people find it.

De Hacker News à mon casque Quest : comment AmblyoPunch a changé ma rééducation visuelle

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Le fil « Ask HN: Who is hiring? (November 2026) » a franchi les 200 points et 166 commentaires. En le lisant, j’ai vu une vague d’offres VR/AR, computer vision et health‑tech. Ça m’a rappelé pourquoi j’avais quitté mon poste de dev backend pour rejoindre Seven Sportz, un petit studio qui mélange graphics engineers, chercheurs en vision binoculaire et game designers.

J’ai lancé AmblyoPunch sur mon Quest 2 un soir d’hiver. La première scène, un village virtuel, m’a demandé de frapper des « Gabor coins » (Gabor patches) et d’esquiver des pics rouges. Chaque coup réussi montait mon score, chaque pic perdu me coûtait un cœur. Le mode automatique m’a guidé à travers quatre stades : warm‑up monocular pour mon œil paresseux (lazy eye), suppression breaking avec l’œil dominant atténué, rebalancing progressif, puis fusion training dans la zone de Panum.

J’ai configuré mon œil droit comme lazy eye, sévérité modérée, strabisme exotropie. Le système exige ≥ 70 % de réussite sur dix actions pour avancer ; sous 40 % je risquais une rétrogradation. Après quelques sessions, mon taux est passé de 55 % à 78 %, et le mode fusion est devenu accessible.

Le jeu gère à la fois les contrôleurs et le hand‑tracking natif, ce qui m’a permis de rester assis sur mon canapé. Chaque monde — village, ville, espace, lune, Mars, Vénus — débloque des cibles plus petites et plus rapides, forçant mon cerveau à fusionner les deux images. Ce passage du monocular au binocular sans patch physique m’a semblé plus naturel que les exercices classiques.

Rappel important : AmblyoPunch est un jeu d’entraînement, pas un dispositif médical, et ne remplace pas un suivi professionnel.

Essayez‑le ici : https://www.meta.com/en-gb/experiences/amblyopunch/1239507485902689/

9 Near-Perfect Dragon Age Replacements For Fans of Fantasy RPGs

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Messy, morally gray RPGs wherever you look

BioWare’s Dragon Age is a fantasy RPG series about warring factions, world-ending threats, and, yes, the occasional dragon – but that’s the first-glance take. Get into one Dragon Age game (start with Origins!) and you’ll see that the true heart of this series is the people you drag into hell with you.

The choices you can make in Dragon Age are messy. Your companions get legitimately attached to you — or not; your dating life can complicate your mission; and innocuous decisions you make in hour one can affect the sweeping political options available to you in the series’ biggest moments. Whether you seek more of that exact mix of party banter, branching loyalty quests, and a world that remembers you, or you’re intrigued by the same bones in a different skin — sci-fi crew instead of fantasy party, perhaps — here are 9 games to add to your queue.

Why you’ll like this if you like Dragon Age: The cast of well-written characters and intricate combat system (along with Larian Studios’ whimsical charm)

Where to play: PS4, PS5, Windows PC, Xbox Series X

Although Divinity: Original Sin and its sequel are set 1,000 years apart, in both, you’re protecting your fantasy world, Rivellon, from a magical energy (Source) and monsters emanating from the series’ overarching Void. But, luckily, you’re not tackling that alone. Each game gives you the opportunity to gather a party of intrepid explorers to save the world, but, more importantly, bicker constantly as you do.

This dynamic within Divinity: Original Sin and especially Divinity: Original Sin 2 makes the characters the true reason for replay after replay. Sure, you’re solving a murder mystery in Divinity: Original Sin and escaping imprisonment, then surviving the wilds in Divinity: Original Sin 2 — but those are details. Wait until you meet Sebille. You’ll either be best friends or unable to stand each other immediately.

Why you’ll like this if you like Dragon Age: It’s a 2002 game that helped form today’s RPG genre of real-feeling companions and branching questlines

Where to play: Windows PC, PS4, PS5, Switch, Windows PC, Xbox Series X

Before Dragon Age, there was Neverwinter Nights — a fellow BioWare title that paved the way straight to Dragon Age. At the start of the game, a fatal plague called the Wailing Death (chill; normal) is striking the city of Neverwinter. You’re in quarantine, but you still need to graduate from the Neverwinter Academy and figure out how to help Lady Aribeth create a cure for the plague. People are panicking. There are riots to navigate. Everything’s absolute chaos behind the city’s closed walls.

Since Neverwinter Nights is nearly a 25-year-old-game, some parts of it are harder to love than others, but it’s worth a play for Dragon Age players seeking a nostalgic title.

Why you’ll like this if you like Dragon Age: It’s got similar-feeling companion depth and combat primed for a party tactician

Where to play: Windows PC, PS5, Xbox Series X, Switch

In Warhammer 40,000: Rogue Trader by Owlcat Games, you’re exploring uncharted space…specifically, the Koronus Expanse. But while the setting differs from mostly land-based Dragon Age, the problems your characters face are similar. Here, you’re navigating a succession crisis and working to claim your place within the Von Valancius family. Much like Dragon Age’s Blight and Darkspawn, Rogue Trader has its share of existential corruption for you to weather (here, it’s Chaos and Warp mutations) while you also have to deal with the possibility of much more immediate betrayal.

Dragon Age players will recognize Rogue Trader’s starting class and character background system – will you be a Warrior who was a military officer, or a priest-turned-Operative? – but the turn-based combat will be a change of pace.

Why you’ll like this if you like Dragon Age: Everywhere you turn, there are fiendishly difficult narrative choices. Buckle up

Where to play: PS4, PS5, Switch, Windows PC, Xbox Series X

Welcome to Baldur’s Gate 2 — surprise, you’re trapped in an underground laboratory. You’re Gorion’s Ward, a Bhaalspawn (e.g., kid of the murder god Bhaal). There’s a devilish wizard, Jon Irenicus, who’s torturing you – ahem, conducting tests to study your essence. You need to escape, rescue your also-trapped childhood friend Imoen, and then rest and recover…as long as nothing happens to interfere with that plan, of course.

In Baldur’s Gate 2, as in the Dragon Age series, you’re learning about the world around you and figuring out who you can trust or recruit to help you accomplish a goal that’s both very personal and potentially world-ending. But here, there are vampires.

Baldur’s Gate 2 is another BioWare title from before the company moved away from D&D, so the two games also share developer DNA. (As a bonus, if you’re a Dragon Age person and you’ve not played Baldur’s Gate 3…please do.)

Why you’ll like this if you like Dragon Age: It’s got the same BioWare choice-and-companion architecture. Just swap your sword for an assault rifle

Where to play: PS4, PS5, Windows PC, Xbox Series X

Mass Effect and Dragon Age are both BioWare RPG franchises with relationship building, storytelling, and party- or squad-based tactics. In each, your choices shape the narrative. In each, you can use save data from one game to influence the next. In each, you have a base — a camp or a spaceship — where you can regroup with your companions.

But that’s where the similarities end. In Mass Effect, you start as newcomer Commander Shepard aboard an advanced starship. You’re working to investigate a beacon on a human colony world…until things may or may not very suddenly take a turn for the worse (brace for attack!). But at least you’re fighting alongside friends. Getting to hang with the sullen, sarcastic Garrus Vakarian might be one of the greatest joys in the series.

Why you’ll like this if you like Dragon Age: It’s got party-based tactics and large-scale kingdom management.

Where to play: PS4, PS5, Windows PC, Xbox Series X

If the political games you could play within Dragon Age: Origins were the draw for you, Pathfinder: Kingmaker has that in spades. With even more management systems layered on top. Here, you start the game as a group of adventurers gathered by one Lady Jamandi Alordi. You’re going to the Stolen Lands to bring back the head of the Stag Lord. If you do, you get to found your own barony. If not…well, let’s not contemplate that alternative, shall we?

While this game isn’t just routine kingdom management – there are mysterious fey threats and an ancient curse getting in the way of your day-to-day – steering Pathfinder: Kingmaker’s plot via being a decent lord to your barony is a big focus in this game. But, if you’re looking for explicitly similar structure to feel anchored here, Dragon Age: Origins players rejoice: the combat is real-time-with-pause, which should feel just like home.

Why you’ll like this if you like Dragon Age: It’s got grudge-holding factions, possibly-loyal companions, within a Colonial-era setting replete with political intrigue.

Where to play: Windows PC, PS4, PS5, Windows PC, Xbox Series X

Less polish, smaller budget, rougher edges, but a lot of the same charm – GreedFall feels like Dragon Age in that, here, you’ve got real-time combat, a world where your choices matter, and the ability to deeply bond (or not) with your companions. In GreedFall, though, instead of battling the Blight, you’re working to save the old continent from a plague, the Malichor. Your character is a Legate tasked with traveling to an island untouched by the plague to discover the cure, but you’re not the only one with that idea. As multiple colonial factions – including the religious nation Thélème and the fond-of-science Bridge Alliance – fight over the island, your job is simple: Protect the island, search for the plague’s cure, and fend off political disaster. That does sound simple, right?

Why you’ll like this if you like Dragon Age: It’s a crunchier, more tactical game: Think Dragon Age run through a true Dungeons & Dragons ruleset.

Where to play: PS5, Windows PC, Xbox Series X

As Solasta: Crown of the Magister begins, your party of four is called upon to investigate the Badlands – a land infested with monsters, but you’re prepared for that, right? You do find the titular Crown of the Magister along the way, an artifact that might just be able to help you save your world.

Dragon Age players who loved the series’ focus on deep character writing may find Solasta a bit lacking. Solasta’s focus is not on story. Instead, you’re managing turn-based combat, organizing inventories, and figuring out how to use your class abilities to their greatest effect.

To the “less of a story” point, though, Solasta’s sequel is in development. It’s theorized that the team’s leading harder into story this time around, so stay tuned.

A weekly cheat sheet to what you’ll eventually love to play. No buying, no commitment — just saving for later.

Why you’ll like this if you like Dragon Age: While this game doesn’t have traditional team management – you’ve got pawns, not true parties – it does have the same dark fantasy tone and ingrained class build system.

Where to play: PS4, PS5, Windows PC, Xbox Series X

A gigantic dragon has just attacked the fishing village of Cassardis. When you step up to defend, the dragon rips out your heart, eats it, and leaves you for dead. Oh no! Game over. Except it’s not: You wake up as an Arisen. You’re now bound to the dragon’s fate. You need to find it and get your heart back. Have fun!

Dragon’s Dogma is very different mechanically from Dragon Age, but it sets a similar scene and feels in-genre anyway. You won’t find deep companion writing here, but what you will find are dragons. Did you play Dragon Age for dragons? Extremely understandable. You’ll love Dragon’s Dogma. We cannot overstate how many dragons there are.

With Dragon Age, BioWare helped build a genre of games where stories and companions come to life on a grand scale – but there’s still the quintessential battle RPG focus on skill progression and party tactics. Interested in saving a fantasy continent from the plague? More interested in building your own barony? Regardless, there’s something here to play. And replay, with different choices. And replay, as an evil overlord. And then replay again, just for fun.

BioWare's swan song now leaves a grand legacy

8 DC Heroes Who Would Be Better Green Lanterns Than Fans Expect

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The Green Lantern Ring is one of the universe’s most powerful weapons, and the Guardians choose their champions based on their ability to overcome great fear. While iconic Green Lanterns such as Hal Jordan and John Stewart are worthy wielders of the ring, the allure of wondering how other heroes would fare is far too great.

The DC universe is full of heroes with the right psychological profiles and traits to make fantastic Green Lanterns. Willpower isn’t in short supply in DC, but some heroes are so fearless, mentally resilient, and willful that they could be even better Green Lanterns than fans realise right away.

This article spotlights eight heroes who would make better Green Lanterns than fans might expect. From street-level heroes to some of DC’s heaviest hitters, these unsung figures have exactly the traits needed to master the ring. They are already formidable, but a Green Lantern ring would unlock an entirely new layer of their potential.

Batman uses fear to keep criminals in check, which makes him a strong candidate for a Yellow Lantern ring, but fans may also not expect the Dark Knight to become a powerful Green Lantern. The animated series The Batman (2004) shows how effective he would be with the ring.

In the series, after Sinestro attacks him, Hal Jordan sends his ring to Gotham City to find Batman. Although the ring initially falls into the Penguin’s hands, it eventually reaches Batman. With the ring on a low charge, Batman fights and defeats Sinestro, showing he is adept at using the Green Lantern ring.

Overall, Batman is one of DC’s best strategists, a master of all kinds of combat, and an extremely disciplined crime fighter. With a lantern ring, Batman can put his strategic mind to good use, turning the ring into the ultimate tactical weapon. When it comes to sheer willpower, very few heroes can match the Caped Crusader.

The Green Lantern Corps has never lacked power, but it has often lacked wisdom at the top. The Guardians have repeatedly made poor decisions with potentially devastating consequences, including creating the Manhunters. As a result, Diana of Themyscira would not only be a powerful addition to the Corps, but also a great leader.

Diana has commanded Amazon armies, anchored the Justice League through its darkest crises, and served as an ambassador between gods and mortals. She is as wise as she is powerful, and her skills would be best used leading the Green Lantern Corps as a member.

Beyond her outstanding leadership and strategic mind, she is also a fierce warrior and a compassionate hero. Diana is a proud Amazon warrior, and bravery and willpower abound on Themyscira. With a Green Lantern ring, she transforms from a hero who can hold her own against Superman into one of the most powerful Green Lanterns.

John Constantine is nothing if not creative. His unprecedented ability to think on his feet and battle powerful otherworldly threats makes him a top-tier Green Lantern. Constantine’s entire career is defined by staring down literal demons, hellish overlords, and cosmic horrors while armed with nothing but raw nerve. It’s not that he’s fearless; he’s just immensely courageous.

His occult expertise could become twice as dangerous when combined with a Lantern ring. His mind could generate ethereal binding runes, hard-light sigils, and metaphysical cages. He could also create devastating constructs of powerful demons and otherworldly beings, crafting intricate esoteric constructs designed specifically to exploit his opponent’s psychological and mystical weaknesses.

Giving a Speedster a Green Lantern ring basically makes them unstoppable. That is even more true when the Speedster is the Flash. Whether it is Barry Allen or Wally West, the Speed Force demands incredible mental fortitude to wield properly. The Flash sees most things in slow motion, giving him plenty of time to create ideal constructs.

Pairing the Green Lantern ring’s ability to operate at the speed of thought with the Flash’s faster-than-light speed thinking means a Flash with a ring could generate millions of unique hard-light constructs instantly. The Flash would have ample time to create constructs that defy the imagination before his enemies can process what is happening.

A Green Lantern ring is limited only by its wielder’s imagination, which makes Jaime Reyes a perfect candidate. The Blue Beetle scarab works much like the Green Lantern ring: it reads intent and creates weapons the user can imagine. Giving Jaime a ring would not only be a power-up, but it’s right in his wheelhouse.

Furthermore, Jaime’s life is defined by constantly resisting the scarab’s lethal programming and standing his ground against cosmic threats. Overcoming the fear of losing control of his body shows immense willpower, but his real advantage is his bond with extraterrestrial technology, which would give his constructs an unimaginable edge.

Instead of generating static shields or basic blades, his mind would produce dynamic, evolving weaponry that mirrors Reach tech. His constructs would instantly adapt to target vulnerabilities, morphing mid-battle, making him a deadly combination of human creativity and machine logic.

When fans think of Raven, they immediately picture shadow magic and the dark, demonic influence of her father, Trigon. However, her entire life has been a constant exercise in mental discipline and emotional control. Holding back absolute evil from consuming her soul takes astonishing willpower, proving her mind is stronger than most people imagine.

Her unique psychological training would give her constructs an unprecedented edge. Coupled with her mastery of magic, Raven would make a uniquely terrifying Green Lantern, able to turn nightmares into reality through powerful constructs. Her intense focus, sharpened by years of keeping inner turmoil at bay, would produce constructs of rare density and absolute stability.

Mister Miracle’s whole existence is defined by resisting the overwhelming, soul-crushing terror of Darkseid and the pits of Apokolips. With impeccable escape artistry and unshakable willpower, he did the unthinkable and escaped Darkseid’s prison and grasp. For Scott Free, surrendering to fear was never an option; his unbreakable determination to break every chain makes him a natural fit for willpower.

Moreover, a Green Lantern ring lets the user turn raw imagination into hard-light constructs, and Scott’s mind is uniquely suited to construct creation. Having mastered every escape technique in the cosmos, Mister Miracle has a creative mind that would lend itself to devastating, intricate constructs.

While Superman is a Symbol of Hope in the DC universe, he also has incredible willpower and is an ideal candidate for a Green Lantern ring. The core requirement of a Green Lantern is not just possessing willpower, but the ability to overcome great fear, and Superman has this in abundance.

The Man of Steel may have unimaginable power; he routinely faces beings strong enough to kill him, such as Darkseid and Doomsday. He never backs away from a confrontation when the world is in danger. Moreover, with a super-genius-level intellect and super-speed cognition, he can create highly detailed and creative constructs.

Furthermore, willpower powers the ring and determines how solid the constructs are. Superman’s willpower and mental discipline, sharpened through years of controlling his immense powers, would make him an incredibly powerful Green Lantern. Earth Green Lanterns usually protect Sector 2814, but Superman already does that and more every day.

‘The Invisible Guest’ Remake Director Danial Rifki on Suharto-Era Fear and Why His New Thriller Is About ‘How to Fight Back’

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Indonesian director Danial Rifki’s remake of Spanish thriller “The Invisible Guest,” which he says was completed in 2023 before exclusivity clauses with HBO held up its release, finally reached the big screen at Jakarta World Cinema.

It is one of three Rifki films at the festival.

The Falcon Pictures production, which reworks Oriol Paulo’s 2016 hit “Contratiempo” for an Indonesian setting, stars Reza Rahadian, Mawar de Jongh and Arifin Putra. It debuted on HBO Max on Aug. 7. Related Stories Murder-House Impostor Thriller 'The Stand-In Family' Wins Mirror Fiction Award at Taiwan Creative Content Fest 'Jimmy' Review: Cornball James Stewart Biopic Gets the Manner, but Not the Man

Rifki tells Variety that the matter has only recently been resolved.

He also has two new features at the festival: romantic comedy “Tua Tua Keladi” and thriller “Sandal Jepit No. 10.”

Set among the older residents of a small village, “Tua Tua Keladi” follows the chaos that breaks out when a chemical leak into the village water supply stirs up desire. Unspoken feelings, long-held confessions and passion come out overnight.

“I was thinking, if they have young energy to live the second life, [it is] the second chance to love,” Rifki says.

He wanted the film to challenge a deep-rooted assumption that older people no longer live with energy. For all the awkwardness of its premise, the film keeps away from eroticism, even with its younger characters.

In the digital era, Rifki says, people can record their sexual behavior openly – “and sometimes that kind of freaks me out because I have a daughter. I’m concerned if my daughter [is exposed to] wild media.”

“So with the young characters, I try to approach them with something pure, not physical,” he says.

“Sandal Jepit No. 10” is a confined-space thriller about the brutal police interrogation of Akbar, a grieving father detained on suspicion of murder.

“Indonesians have trauma in politics. Life back in the days of Orde Baru – the New Order in the Suharto era – for around 32 years, we could not criticize the government,” Rifki says. “Now we have the Reformation Era, but it might be happening underground. On social media, if you criticize the government, they will shadowban [your comment].”

“We are the low class of society with this kind of fear that, if we do something, someone with power will oppress us. So the movie is about how to fight back,” he says.

“If you have social media, netizens are here to help you out. People power now is turning [to] a digital era so we can gain that power again. I’m still sure that [there are] many good people,” he adds.

Rifki says the film grew out of his concern over abuse of power in Indonesia today.

“[When I was] a film student, I really wanted to try how to speak in rom-com, thriller, drama, and other genres. [I’m] an evolving and growing filmmaker still in the making,” he says.

The backbone of all his work, he says, is human moral conflict, whatever the genre. He is also keen to try action and horror.

His next film, “Before Thirty,” is set for release in January 2027. Rifki calls it a “survival drama” about a 29-year-old man whose life is failing.

“In Indonesia, people always ask: what is your achievement before 30? Young people are punished by that value that you have to be successful before the age of 30,” Rifki says.

The film, he says, is his “commentary on that issue.”

NYT Connections hints today: Clues, answers for November 8, 2026

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The NYT Connections puzzle today is not too difficult if you're a gym rat.

Connections is the one of the most popular New York Times word games that's captured the public's attention. The game is all about finding the "common threads between words." And just like Wordle, Connections resets after midnight and each new set of words gets trickier and trickier—so we've served up some hints and tips to get you over the hurdle.

If you just want to be told today's puzzle, you can jump to the end of this article for today's Connections solution. But if you'd rather solve it yourself, keep reading for some clues, tips, and strategies to assist you.

The NYT's latest daily word game has become a social media hit. The Times credits associate puzzle editor Wyna Liu with helping to create the new word game and bringing it to the publications' Games section. Connections can be played on both web browsers and mobile devices and require players to group four words that share something in common.

Each puzzle features 16 words and each grouping of words is split into four categories. These sets could comprise of anything from book titles, software, country names, etc. Even though multiple words will seem like they fit together, there's only one correct answer.

If a player gets all four words in a set correct, those words are removed from the board. Guess wrong and it counts as a mistake—players get up to four mistakes until the game ends.

Players can also rearrange and shuffle the board to make spotting connections easier. Additionally, each group is color-coded with yellow being the easiest, followed by green, blue, and purple. Like Wordle, you can share the results with your friends on social media.

NYT Strands hints, answers for November 8, 2026

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Today's NYT Strands hints will be easy if you can easily defend yourself.

Strands, the New York Times' elevated word-search game, requires the player to perform a twist on the classic word search. Words can be made from linked letters — up, down, left, right, or diagonal, but words can also change direction, resulting in quirky shapes and patterns. Every single letter in the grid will be part of an answer. There's always a theme linking every solution, along with the "spangram," a special, word or phrase that sums up that day's theme, and spans the entire grid horizontally or vertically.

By providing an opaque hint and not providing the word list, Strands creates a brain-teasing game that takes a little longer to play than its other games, like Wordle and Connections.

If you're feeling stuck or just don't have 10 or more minutes to figure out today's puzzle, we've got all the NYT Strands hints for today's puzzle you need to progress at your preferred pace.

Today's NYT Strands spangram is horizontal.

Wordle today: Answer, hints for November 8, 2026

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Today's Wordle answer should be easy to solve if you're competitive.

If you just want to be told today's word, you can jump to the bottom of this article for today's Wordle solution revealed. But if you'd rather solve it yourself, keep reading for some clues, tips, and strategies to assist you.

Originally created by engineer Josh Wardle as a gift for his partner, Wordle rapidly spread to become an international phenomenon, with thousands of people around the globe playing every day. Alternate Wordle versions created by fans also sprang up, including battle royale Squabble, music identification game Heardle, and variations like Dordle and Quordle that make you guess multiple words at once.

Wordle eventually became so popular that it was purchased by the New York Times, and TikTok creators even livestream themselves playing.

The best Wordle starting word is the one that speaks to you. But if you prefer to be strategic in your approach, we have a few ideas to help you pick a word that might help you find the solution faster. One tip is to select a word that includes at least two different vowels, plus some common consonants like S, T, R, or N.

It might feel like Wordle is getting harder, but it actually isn't any more difficult than when it first began. You can turn on Wordle's Hard Mode if you're after more of a challenge, though.

Hurdle hints and answers for November 8, 2026

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

If you like playing daily word games like Wordle, then Hurdle is a great game to add to your routine.

There are five rounds to the game. The first round sees you trying to guess the word, with correct, misplaced, and incorrect letters shown in each guess. If you guess the correct answer, it'll take you to the next hurdle, providing the answer to the last hurdle as your first guess. This can give you several clues or none, depending on the words. For the final hurdle, every correct answer from previous hurdles is shown, with correct and misplaced letters clearly shown.

An important note is that the number of times a letter is highlighted from previous guesses does necessarily indicate the number of times that letter appears in the final hurdle.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/health-tech/hims-hers-inks-deal-eli-lilly-offer-branded-weight-loss-drugs" hreflang="en">Hims &amp; Hers inks deal with Eli Lilly to offer branded weight loss drugs</a>

📅 2026-11-06  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →
Hims & Hers inks deal with Eli Lilly to offer branded weight loss drugs" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Hims & Hers struck a deal with Eli Lilly to offer the drugmaker’s branded obesity treatments through its online health and wellness platform.

Under the agreement, Eli Lilly will give Hims & Hers pharmacies direct access to its U.S. Food and Drug Administration-approved GLP-1 medications at self-pay prices. Eligible patients on the Hims & Hers platform can be prescribed and order Eli Lilly's injectable Zepbound (tirzepatide) and daily oral medication Foundayo (orforglipron) without leaving the company's platform.

Prior to this agreement, Lilly drugs were available to patients on Hims & Hers only if they left the site and went to Lilly's direct-to-consumer platform, LillyDirect.

Shares of Hims & Hers jumped 8% Friday following news of the Eli Lilly partnership.

The agreement brings together Lilly’s obesity medicines and Hims & Hers’ consumer platform to expand consumers' access to FDA-approved weight loss drugs, the companies said.

"Patients can complete a consultation, receive a prescription if their provider determines it is appropriate and have an FDA-approved Lilly medicine shipped directly to their home, all in one seamless experience," the companies said in a press release.

Self-pay pricing starts at $149 per month for Foundayo and $299 per month for Zepbound.

"At Lilly, we are always looking for new ways to help more people get the medicines they need, and that means meeting patients where they already are," said Ilya Yuffa, executive vice president and president, Lilly USA and global customer capabilities, Eli Lilly and Company, in a statement. "Millions of people turn to Hims & Hers for their care, and through this agreement, those patients now have a simple, more direct way to get FDA-approved Lilly medicine at an affordable price if prescribed."

Hims & Hers' technology platform supports provider outreach, follow-up care and treatment planning. Weight loss patients on Hims & Hers receive a membership experience that includes 24/7 access to a care team, nutrition guidance, regular clinical check-ins and access to the Hims & Hers Weight Loss community, according to the company.

The agreement with Eli Lilly mirrors Hims & Hers' partnership with Novo Nordisk, which has been selling its branded weight loss drugs Wegovy and Ozempic through Hims & Hers since March. Novo had previously sued the telehealth company for offering compounded versions of its medications. Hims & Hers' agreement with Novo marked a shift in its obesity strategy toward FDA-approved branded GLP-1 treatments and away from cheaper compounded GLP-1 drugs.

Earlier this year, Hims & Hers took heat from the pharmaceutical manufacturer and federal regulators after announcing plans to launch a knockoff of Novo's oral pill. It backed down from those plans under pressure.

Hims & Hers stated that it never offered access to compounded copies of Lilly’s GLP-1 drugs and has committed not to do so in the future.

As part of the agreement, the companies have committed to protecting independent clinical decision-making, so that providers on the Hims & Hers platform continue to prescribe the treatment they determine is right for each patient.

“Lilly is one of the most innovative pharmaceutical companies in history, and I’m incredibly proud that Hims & Hers is a part of making sure more people can access the medicines they’ve created,” said Andrew Dudum, co-founder and CEO of Hims & Hers, in a statement. “By working together, we’re not just reaching more people with obesity with seamless, personal access to FDA-approved Lilly medicine. We’re showing the industry what innovators can achieve for customers everywhere when we work together to make the world well.”

Hims & Hers has faced financial pressure as it shifts from high-margin compounded GLP-1s to lower-margin branded weight-loss drugs. The company posted a $92 million loss in Q1, including $33 million in restructuring costs, largely from write-downs of its compounded GLP-1 supply chain, which executives said faced “risk of obsolescence.”

In Q2, Hims & Hers reported an $86 million loss, compared with a $43 million profit a year earlier. The quarter included $81 million in one-time costs, including expenses tied to its exit from compounded GLP-1s.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/finance/omada-health-hikes-2026-outlook-after-strongest-quarter-ever-q3" hreflang="en">Omada Health hikes 2026 outlook after ‘strongest quarter ever’ in Q3</a>

📅 2026-11-06  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →
Omada Health hikes 2026 outlook after ‘strongest quarter ever’ in Q3" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Omada Health saw its "strongest quarter ever" in the third quarter of 2026, CEO and co-founder Sean Duffy told investors and analysts during a Thursday evening earnings call.

"We delivered our highest quarterly revenue, gross margin progression, and our most profitable quarter to date," Duffy said.

The virtual chronic care provider, which went public last June, brought in $91 million in revenue—up 34% year-over-year—and reported gross margin of 76% on a GAAP basis and 78% on a non-GAAP basis, according to Duffy. Omada reported net income of $7 million in the third quarter, compared with a net loss of $3 million in Q3 2025.

The company also reported adjusted EBITDA of $12 million, compared to $2 million the year prior.

It beat Wall Street analysts’ estimates for revenue, though slightly trailed behind projections for total membership.

Total members reached 1.1 million, up 36% year-over-year, with trailing 12-month revenue per total member increasing 5% year-over-year to $294, according to financial results published Thursday. Average member tenure also grew 10% compared to the year prior, to approximately two years and four months. Member retention has improved to 58%, up from 43% in Q4 of 2024, executives said.

Omada Health President Wei-Li Shao said Q3 membership growth reflects "continued demand" for its cardiometabolic platform, adding that the company's diabetes and hypertension programs continued to lead growth.

The company's diabetes and hypertension programs, longer member retention and lower care delivery costs are increasingly driving both growth and profitability, executives said.

"Members stayed with us longer, and our ability to provide personalized care at scale reached a new level of efficiency," Shao said.

The company supports diabetes, hypertension, obesity and musculoskeletal conditions—launching a GLP-1 care companion solution last November.

Shao noted that Omada deepened its partnership with the Health Care Services Corporation (HCSC) last quarter to extend its prevention and hypertension programs into three additional states, which is expected to "potentially reach an additional estimated 1.5 million covered lives at launch in 2027."

"On the PBM side, we have commercial relationships with the three largest PBMs in the country, which together processed an estimated 80% of prescription claims in 2025," Shao said. "Our prescribing program is in the market now at Optum. These relationships give us the broadest distribution in Omada’s history for our programs."

Shao, who will take over as CEO Jan. 1, also noted that artificial intelligence solutions enable care teams to have more capacity to "apply their clinical expertise" while tailoring care to each member’s needs.

"This quarter shows what we have built at Omada," Shao said. "Our commercial relationships give us broad reach, and our cardiometabolic programs have driven growth. Our program mix has supported longer member tenure, and our cost to serve has decreased."

Chief Financial Officer Steve Cook told investors the company expects new enrollments to "moderate sequentially" in the fourth quarter, which he said is "consistent with historic seasonality."

Shao also noted that a number of employers have been spending more time making GLP-1 coverage and obesity care decisions, "given the size of the commitment, the range of solutions and the importance of getting these decisions right," which has extended the selling season deeper into the fourth quarter.

Omada Health's stock dropped 19% Friday following the Q3 earnings call on Thursday as investors pulled back due to concerns about the longer selling season and expected Q4 member decline clouding the company's 2027 outlook.

The company is raising its full 2026 revenue and adjusted EBITDA outlook. Cook said revenue is expected to range from $344 million to $346 million, up from its previous range of $334 million to $340 million. Adjusted EBITDA is expected to be in the range of $32 million to $34 million, up from $21 million to $27 million.

"Our outlook anticipates Q4 revenue below our Q3 level, reflecting the lower new enrollment and engagement activity we have historically seen during the holidays," Cook said. "We expect revenue to come primarily from continued engagement among existing members."

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/ai-and-machine-learning/equiti-acquires-lexi-ai-combine-ai-human-medical-interpretation" hreflang="en">Equiti acquires Lexi AI to combine AI, human medical interpretation</a>

📅 2026-11-06  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →
Equiti acquires Lexi AI to combine AI, human medical interpretation" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Equiti is acquiring Lexi AI to build a hybrid language-access model that combines AI language support with human medical interpreters.

Equiti, which sells medical interpretation services to hospitals and health systems through its Martti business, provides 24/7 access to qualified medical interpreters across more than 250 languages and dialects. Lexi provides multilingual, AI-powered medical interpretation. The acquisition deal merges Lexi's immediate AI language support, covering over 35 languages and dialects, with Martti and its network of qualified medical interpreters to help healthcare organizations expand language access.

The acquisition was announced October 28. Financial details of the deal were not disclosed.

With this acquisition, Equiti is building on Martti’s work pioneering video remote interpretation in healthcare more than two decades ago, according to executives. The use of AI can expand where and when language services can be offered, particularly for everyday interactions that may not currently receive interpretation support, Equiti executives said.

Martti was built alongside healthcare organizations to make qualified interpreters available by video. Lexi, in turn, has been developed with healthcare customers to extend immediate language support into more workflows. The acquisition brings those capabilities together, with human-in-the-loop design, patient safety and patient experience at its center, Equiti executives said.

The company saw an opportunity to combine its services with Lexi's AI expertise rather than build a healthcare AI interpretation product internally, Maureen Huber, Equiti CEO, told Fierce Healthcare.

"Lexi brings specialized expertise in AI, engineering and healthcare product design, along with experience putting that technology into the hands of care teams. That combination takes time to build," Huber said. "For more than 20 years, our work through Martti has been about making qualified human interpretation accessible within healthcare. We understand the service, the clinical environment and what it takes to support healthcare organizations every day. Developing AI and machine learning technology calls for additional, specialized expertise. We wanted people who had already been doing that work with healthcare in mind."

She added, "We spent time evaluating the category and learning from customers. Lexi brought together the people, product and approach we were seeking. Combining their expertise with ours gives us a stronger foundation to expand language access responsibly."

Combining AI and human interpretation makes language support more accessible across the patient journey, helping organizations improve care delivery while making better use of their resources, according to Equiti executives.

Healthcare organizations are increasingly interested in AI interpretation capabilities and are looking for guidance, Huber noted.

"Customers were asking: 'Where could AI help? How do we evaluate it? What is appropriate for our patients?' Healthcare leaders see the same advances in consumer AI that everyone else sees. They also must think about what happens when those tools enter a care environment, sometimes before an organization has decided how they should be used. They want a partner who understands both the possibilities and the responsibilities," she said.

She added, "I would describe that as inquiry and evaluation. There is real curiosity about extending language support to more of the patient experience, particularly before and after a visit. Our job is to help customers identify those opportunities while keeping qualified human interpretation central to care delivery."

Equiti calls this approach Modality Matters, a connected model that allows healthcare organizations to match each encounter with the appropriate modality — immediate, healthcare-purpose-built AI support for approved use cases and qualified medical interpretation where clinical complexity, regulatory requirements, organizational policy or patient preference calls for a human interpreter. With this approach, organizations have the flexibility to shift between modalities as clinical context and communication needs change in real time.

The combined portfolio of Equiti and Lexi's capabilities will extend language access into everyday interactions that often go unsupported, Huber noted.

Equiti is focusing Lexi's AI-powered medical interpretation on routine patient engagement, including before and after care, such as appointment communications, scheduling and nonclinical follow-up logistics.

"We see a significant opportunity for Lexi in that communication before and after care: helping patients exchange messages, arrange appointments and stay connected in their preferred language. Language access must extend beyond the clinical encounter. Think about what happens after a patient leaves the clinic. They receive a message about a follow-up appointment, but they need to ask whether it is at the same location, how to reschedule, or whom to contact. Those may be simple questions for the organization, but the answers determine whether the patient can take the next step," she said.

Qualified human interpreters remain the foundation for care delivery, Huber noted. "In conversations such as those about a diagnosis, informed consent, treatment or palliative care, the ability to clarify meaning, recognize cultural context and respond with empathy is essential. A medically qualified interpreter helps people understand one another through conversations that can be complex and deeply personal," she said.

Equiti said it can work with customers to define appropriate uses within their policies and applicable requirements, preserving patient choice and access to a qualified human interpreter.

Language access is also a strategic performance driver for healthcare organizations, as effective communication influences patient understanding and experience, clinical outcomes, workforce efficiency and financial performance.

Equiti's customers include Wellstar Health System, Corewell Health, NYU Langone and Lee Health. Lexi works with a growing pipeline of federally qualified health centers and community providers including Lowell Community Health Center, which serves one of the most multilingual communities in Massachusetts.

As general-purpose AI gets better at translation, a healthcare-specific interpretation platform has distinct advantages, Huber asserted.

"Healthcare communication involves clinical meaning, cultural context, jargon and acronyms during situations where people may be frightened, vulnerable or unsure how to ask a question. Our job is to remove those communication barriers, and that requires understanding the care environment, fitting into clinical workflows and knowing when a qualified human interpreter needs to be involved," she said. "That is where Equiti’s experience matters. We bring the relationships, service infrastructure and human expertise developed through more than two decades of healthcare interpretation, alongside Lexi’s technology and team."

Huber said she envisions broader uses of Lexi's AI language support in healthcare workflows. "A patient’s need to communicate continues after the conversation with a clinician ends. Lexi already supports text-based messaging, including translation of text provided by a care team, and can even leave voicemail messages for patients. That creates opportunities to help patients access information and communicate between visits. Helping someone navigate follow-up care is a natural direction for that work, like understanding the next step, connecting with the right office or arranging an appointment. The value is in making it easier for patients to act on the care plan they have been given and ask for help along the way," she said.

"We built Lexi to fit the way healthcare teams actually work, bringing immediate language support into the devices and workflows they already use,” Siddharth UR, co-founder and chief product officer of Lexi, said in a statement. “As part of Equiti, we can extend that experience across the patient journey and connect it to a broader language access model built around the needs of each encounter.”

Lexi will operate alongside Martti under Equiti, with the teams working together on implementation and integration across healthcare workflows. Equiti will work with each customer to determine how the solutions fit within its language access program, clinical environment, and organizational policies.

« D’habitude, j’ai beaucoup à dire pour défendre notre équipe, mais pas ce soir » : Ruben Amorim accable ses joueurs après le fiasco de l'AC Milan

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Quatre jours après le succès dans le derby milanais, l'AC Milan a sombré à domicile face à Ferencváros en Ligue Europa. Excédé par le visage affiché par son équipe, l'entraîneur portugais Ruben Amorim a recadré ses joueurs en conférence de presse d'après-match.

Le jeudi 5 novembre 2026, San Siro a été le théâtre d'une immense désillusion pour les Rossoneri, battus (0-2) par la formation hongroise lors de la 4e journée de la phase de ligue. Une prestation indigne des ambitions lombardes qui place l'AC Milan dans une situation délicate sur la scène européenne.

Face aux journalistes à l'issue du coup de sifflet final, le technicien portugais n'a pas mâché ses mots au moment d'analyser la copie rendue par ses hommes. Ruben Amorim a notamment qualifié la première période d'« affreuse », refusant catégoriquement de chercher la moindre circonstance atténuante à ce défaite devant le public milanais. Le ton est donné.

Pourtant galvanisé par sa prestigieuse victoire face à l'Inter (3-1) en Serie A le week-end précédent, le club lombard a déçu. Ruben Amorim avait procédé à une large rotation dans son onze de départ, où seuls Ramos, Rabiot, Musah, De Winter et Diawara avaient débuté le choc face aux Nerazzurri. Les Milanais ont craqué au retour des vestiaires sur une grave erreur de contrôle du gardien de 21 ans Lorenzo Torriani, aligné pour suppléer Mike Maignan suspendu, sur une passe en retrait du défenseur Koni De Winter sous la pression de Kristoffer Zachariassen. L'attaquant haïtien Lenny Joseph en a profité pour ouvrir le score à la 52e minute, avant de signer un doublé à la 66e minute.

L'expulsion directe de Yusuf Bamidele à la 77e minute pour un coup de coude sur Mario Gila a laissé Ferencváros en infériorité numérique pour le dernier quart d'heure, sans que Milan ne parvienne à réduire l'écart. Les entrées en jeu en cours de rencontre de Diego Moreira, Christian Pulisic et Luka Modric n'ont pas permis aux Milanais d'inverser le cours du match. En zone mixte après la rencontre, Koni De Winter a d'ailleurs partagé le constat général : « Ce sont encore ces moments de perte de concentration. Aujourd’hui (hier), rien n’allait du tout. Je ne peux pas l’expliquer. Tout est une question de mental : la façon dont nous abordons les matchs, la façon dont nous jouons. C’est notre point faible ».

Au-delà de la défaite, la situation comptable devient particulièrement alarmante pour les Milanais, qui pointent désormais à une inquiétante 28e place au classement général de la Ligue Europa avec seulement 3 points en quatre rencontres, soit une victoire et trois défaites. Pour tenter de redresser la barre, Ruben Amorim a insisté sur le manque de culture de travail et a érigé Strahinja Pavlović en modèle d'engagement, estimant que tout le monde devrait s'entraîner comme lui.

L'AC Milan tentera de réagir en Serie A lors de son prochain déplacement sur la pelouse du Genoa, programmé le dimanche 8 novembre 2026 à 15h00 au stade Luigi Ferraris.

Set up model-radar: catch free LLMs that really make tool calls

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

model-radar is two small commands I run for my AI agents:

Neither asks a model for an opinion. They measure and act. Setup is about ten minutes, and the guide is run on a fresh Ubuntu machine every week.

Python 3.11 or newer, nothing to pip install.

Before adding a key, run both with no credentials:

That exit 1 matters. If the canary can't check anything, a timer should notice, not shrug.

One line per model: PASS, FAIL or BUSY. BUSY means rate-limited or timed out, and it's never treated as a verdict, it just gets tried next run. On 8 October that gave me 4 passes out of 8 probed, two 403s and two busy. One free model I'd tried earlier answered every request and never once called the tool.

Run it once without --dry-run to save a baseline. From then on it only probes models it hasn't seen.

Point it at whatever your agents use (a JSON file, or hermes-agent profiles), add a paid fallback ladder and a local model in config.toml, and run it every few hours from cron. Two hard failures in a row and it walks the ladder. Timeouts and 429s never count.

Anything with a key. The weekly run has none, so it proves install, config, help and the no-key behaviour. The probes and the canary's failover need your OpenRouter account.

Full guide: github.com/casareanderson/model-radar/blob/main/docs/SETUP.md

How do you pick models for your agents, and how do you find out when one quietly stops working?
