‘Kamen Rider’ Studio Toei Frames Generative AI Rules, Sets First Video Games for 2027

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Toei, the Japanese studio giant behind “Kamen Rider” and the Super Sentai series that became “Power Rangers,” has drawn up rules for generative AI and started using AI in its film and video production, president and CEO Yoshimura Fumio said at the Taiwan Creative Content Fest (TCCF) in Taipei.

A dedicated team at the company is testing how far AI can be used in actual content production, and Toei is building an AI system that helps less experienced producers estimate budgets. Related Stories Robert Pattinson to Receive 40th American Cinematheque Award A Version of Zendaya's Viral 'Spider-Man' Tee Is Now Available to Shop Online

“We have put in place rules for the use of generative AI, and from this year we have started using AI in our film and video production,” Yoshimura said. “We are already working on things like creating backgrounds with AI.”

Toei’s back-office departments also use AI for routine tasks, data processing and internal queries, and the company is looking at applying it to the creative supervision of IP merchandise. Yoshimura pointed to “Dreams of Violets,” a live-action feature made entirely with generative AI that was selected for the Tribeca Festival in June. The 75-minute film was reportedly made by two creators for around $2,000.

“I believe AI is not something that takes away jobs, but a tool that expands the possibilities of expression and business,” he said.

Toei also put a timeline on its move into games. Toei Games, the label launched this year to mark the company’s 75th anniversary, will release “Hino” in early 2027, with “Killa” and “Debug Nephemee” following from spring 2027. All three are original titles from outside creators rather than adaptations of Toei IP, and will launch on Steam before rolling out to Nintendo Switch, PlayStation and Xbox.

“We aim to bring game creators’ talent on board over the long term as a new vein of IP creation, and make games the starting point for stories in place of novels and comics,” Yoshimura said.

The keynote also mapped out how Toei is taking “Kamen Rider,” which turned 55 this year, beyond Japanese TV. Yoshimura held up the franchise as his main example of what the company calls the “evergreening” of IP. The first series premiered in 1971, and the Showa-era shows that ran until 1988 built its core fanbase. The Heisei-era series that began in 2000 handed the franchise down from parents to children, while events, merchandise, manga, anime and stage shows have since carried it to a third generation.

The current series, “Kamen Rider Zeztz,” was rolled out almost simultaneously around the world, including Taiwan, the rest of Asia and North America, which Yoshimura called a big step for the group. Toei is also pushing into India. “Kamen Rider” was introduced there at a Japanese IP event in 2025, and the strong response led to a deal with a local Disney-affiliated company.

“Considering the population size and rising incomes from a growing middle class, I think India can be called an extremely promising market in the medium to long term,” Yoshimura said. “We expect it will take time before this takes root as a business, but it’s important to build connections now and steadily grow our partnerships and fan base.”

In April, Toei launched The Kamen Rider Animated, an animation label. Its first project teams the studio with Aniplex, the company behind “Demon Slayer,” and Shirogumi, which worked on Yamazaki Takashi’s “Godzilla Minus One,” on a new “Kamen Rider” property with a scale and style of action only animation can deliver. A live-action label, The Kamen Rider Premium, will make full-scale hero action films for a wider audience, including international viewers and people who don’t follow the TV shows.

Under its Toei New Wave 2033 medium- to long-term plan, the company aims to raise the share of its sales that comes from overseas from 30% to 50%, with Asia the current focus. Yoshimura cited estimates that the global entertainment and media market is growing by an average of 3.4% a year. He added that streaming now lets K-pop, Japanese anime, Indian films and Chinese microdramas travel the world without being changed.

“Now that content rooted in local cultures and histories, like ‘Shogun,’ is crossing racial and national borders and captivating people around the world, I’d say the main battlefield for our business is overseas,” he said.

During the audience Q&A, Yoshimura said that before “Power Rangers,” Toei exported its Japanese series unchanged, and they struggled to win over Western audiences. The breakthrough came from keeping only the characters and rebuilding the cast and drama in the U.S.

“I believe this approach of localization is indispensable for spreading characters around the world,” he said. “We also understand that, unlike animation, live-action work in particular is not something that is readily accepted as-is in each country.”

“Power Rangers” has now run in the U.S. for more than 30 years. In 2025, the original “Mighty Morphin Power Rangers” was relaunched as “Mighty Morphin Power Rangers: Re-Ignition,” with newly designed toys from Hasbro, a “Fortnite” collaboration and a Lego line in the works. Toei used a similar remake model in the Philippines. “Voltes V: Legacy,” the 2023 live-action version of Toei’s 1977 anime “Super Electromagnetic Machine Voltes V,” peaked at a 58% rating there and was released in Japanese cinemas the following year.

The company is also building stories from scratch in other markets. In February, it signed a strategic co-production partnership with Thai producer-distributor M Studio. Together with M Studio and Korea’s Showbox, it is adapting Ito Junji’s “Long Hair in the Attic” as a premium Thai film for Asian audiences. The film will open in Thailand first before heading to Japan and other territories.

In co-production, Toei backed “Dear Stranger,” a Japan-Taiwan-U.S. film from director Mariko Tetsuya that stars Gwei Lun-mei and Nishijima Hidetoshi. It was shot entirely in New York and released last September. The project was selected for the Hong Kong Asia Film Financing Forum’s HAF and raised money abroad from the start, with Taiwanese investors including TAICCA’s TICP 2.0 program. Toei’s Kyoto studio is also supporting the Japan shoot of “Iris,” an action thriller from a U.S. independent producer.

Toei is the only company in Japan that owns and runs two studio lots, one in Tokyo and one in Kyoto. The Tokyo lot houses Japan’s only permanent 270-degree LED volume stage.

Toei is also making short-form content. “Everyday Host Club” is a short anime of 24 episodes that run about three minutes each. “Meshimori Onna,” produced at the Kyoto studio for a client, is a vertical period drama of 30 episodes that run one to two minutes. Yoshimura suggested that vertical formats could one day become the norm even in cinemas.

On Taiwan, he noted that in 2025 Japanese films were the most-released titles in the market and second only to U.S. films at the box office. Toei titles released there include “Midnight Diner,” “Shin Kamen Rider,” “Howling Village” and “Oshi no Ko.” Local broadcaster YoYo TV airs “Kamen Rider,” and Toei is expanding live character shows in Taiwan with a local partner. The studio also invested in the Taiwanese horror film “The Rope Curse 4.” Yoshimura said he wants long-term tokusatsu partnerships and international co-productions with Taiwan.

“We are looking for partners with whom we can plan, make and deliver compelling stories to the world together,” he said.

Yoshimura joined Toei in 1988. He worked in live events, licensing and digital distribution, including some of Japan’s early mobile content and subscription streaming services, and became president in 2023.

FA and WSL Football launch major study into ACL injuries in women’s football

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

To help deliver timely coverage of women's football, we may use AI tools to support content creation and editing. While we strive for accuracy and editorial quality, readers should independently verify important information.

The Football Association and WSL Football have teamed up with Imperial College London on a new study into ACL injuries, the knee problem that has cost the women’s game some of its biggest names in recent seasons. All clubs across the Barclays WSL and WSL2 are being asked to take part, from the players themselves to the medical, performance and coaching staff around them.

Announced on Thursday, the project is being run as a PhD programme funded by the FA. Imperial researcher Hanna Lehtovirta, based in the university’s Department of Mechanical Engineering, is leading the work, with Fortius Clinic knee surgeon Simon Ball also involved. According to the FA’s announcement, the aim is to find out why anterior cruciate ligament ruptures keep happening in the elite women’s game and to turn those findings into practical prevention advice.

The research is split into two stages. In the first, players complete surveys covering their injury record, how much they train and play, and hormonal, nutritional and psychological factors. Anyone who has ruptured an ACL since the 2018-19 campaign will have their answers set against those of players who have avoided the injury, in the hope of spotting patterns linked to risk.

The second stage goes back to the same group for interviews, and this time staff are brought in too. The FA says plenty of earlier research has looked at single factors, often in a lab, and that far less is known about how they combine in the daily routine of a professional squad. Lehtovirta said her team would be looking at everything “from minutes played and injury prevention practices at clubs to how players developed before they entered professional football.”

ESPN reports that data gathering began in June and that the study is set to run for three years.

The numbers explain the urgency. Research cited by ESPN suggests women are around twice as likely as men to suffer an ACL injury, and roughly two in every three cases happen with no contact from another player. England captain Leah Williamson and Beth Mead both missed the 2023 World Cup after rupturing the ligament, and Vivianne Miedema has also had to come back from the injury during her time in England.

Ritan Mehta, the FA’s Head of Women’s Medical, was clear about the scale of the problem. “ACL injuries remain one of the biggest challenges facing female footballers,” she said. “While significant progress has been made in understanding the factors that may contribute to these injuries, there is still much more to learn.”

WSL Football’s Medical Governance Lead, Dr Abosede Ajayi, pointed to the mix of football, healthcare and academia behind the project, saying the findings could “inform medical standards across the women’s game”. Ball put it more bluntly: “ACL injury is devastating for professional athletes,” he said, adding that “prevention is better than cure!”

For more on how ACL injuries have hit the women’s game, read our reports Spurs confirm record signing Alice Sombath has ruptured her ACL and Project ACL puts WSL injury crisis back in focus with cross-league research.

The English study follows a similar move in the United States, where the NWSL and its players’ association joined Project ACL in April. The FA also marked the launch on social media:

Together with the @BarclaysWSL, we have launched a new research project to better understand ACL injuries in women’s football.

Bitcoin, Ether ETFs post $530M outflows as election gains evaporate

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

US spot Bitcoin and Ether ETFs shed a combined $530 million Thursday as Bitcoin fell below $76,000 and US stocks tumbled.

US spot Bitcoin and Ether exchange-traded funds (ETFs) posted about $530 million in net outflows Thursday as cryptocurrency markets fell sharply.

Bitcoin (BTC) ETFs recorded $430 million in net outflows while Ether (ETH) funds lost around $100 million, according to SoSoValue data.

The reversal followed $128 million in combined inflows Wednesday, when Bitcoin briefly climbed above $80,000 after Democrats’ victories in the US midterm elections.

Bitcoin fell back below $76,000 and the tech-heavy Nasdaq Composite plunged 5.5% on Thursday, which was its biggest single-day decline since April 2025, according to Reuters.

Fidelity’s Wise Origin Bitcoin Fund (FBTC) led Thursday’s withdrawals with roughly $200 million in outflows after attracting around $237 million on Wednesday, according to Farside Investors data.

BlackRock’s iShares Bitcoin Trust (IBIT) recorded another $105 million in net outflows. The latest withdrawals extended IBIT’s outflow streak to five consecutive sessions, with more than $1 billion withdrawn since Oct. 30.

Daily spot Bitcoin ETF flows by issuer since Oct. 30. Source: Farside Investors

Bitcoin ETFs’ year-to-date net outflows stood at around $510 million following Thursday’s closing bell.

Crypto analyst Michaël van de Poppe said on X Thursday that Bitcoin could find a near-term bottom Friday, potentially after dipping below $75,000, pointing to the severity of the Nasdaq’s decline.

“I expect Nasdaq to bottom tomorrow. The same for Bitcoin, potentially after sweeping $75,000,” he wrote.

US stock market heat map. Source: Yahoo Finance

Dean Lyulkin, CEO of Cardiff and founder of investment publication The Dean’s List, said the current market environment differs from the period leading up to the 2008 financial crisis.

Related: Bitcoin crashes 6% to 7-week low as US stocks erase election gains

“The global financial crisis wasn’t caused by Democrats taking Congress,” Lyulkin said in comments shared with Cointelegraph, noting that the Federal Reserve had already raised interest rates substantially and the housing market was weakening before the crisis.

While acknowledging vulnerabilities in commercial real estate, private credit and potentially excessive artificial intelligence spending, Lyulkin argued that household balance sheets and the banking system are considerably stronger today.

Is the Gyokeres iron hot enough for Hull?

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

I’m here for our book launch which takes place tonight, but also for our game against Hull tomorrow. Mikel Arteta will meet the press this afternoon ahead of that one, and we’ll bring you all the stories on Arseblog News. I don’t think there’s gonna be anything mind-blowing, it looked like we came through the Slavia Prague game without any issues, so fingers crossed that’s the case.

I suppose I’m mildly curious as to whether Viktor Gyokeres’ performance on Wednesday will see him keep his place up front. He got a great goal, provided an assist for Noni Madueke, and probably should have had another assist when he headed back across goal for Mikel Merino, only for the Spaniard to head over the bar when we know he can do better from those situations.

He’s a player who seems to need confidence and rhythm, and on that basis it would make some sense to keep him in the starting line-up. After the last Interlull, when he scored in four successive games for Sweden, I made the case that giving him a start in our first game back would make some sense to try and harness some of what was a good goal scoring run. Strike while the iron is hot and all that.

I looked at a home game against Leeds as the kind of fixture that would suit him. He scored two against them at home last season, and one at Elland Road, but when the time came Mikel Arteta picked Kai Havertz up front. Speaking about the decision to start Gyokeres on Wednesday in Prague, the manager said:

It was a game we believed would be really good for him, for the demands the opponent puts in and the spaces he could use. He’s done it really well, not just for the assist and the goal, but he was really connected to the team. It’s really great news for him, because he really needed that performance.

The question will be does Arteta think Hull at home will be as good for him as Slavia Prague away? After a bright start to the season they haven’t won any of their last seven but they have drawn five of those. Chances are they’re going to come and sit deep, using the blueprint we’ve seen many teams deploy against Arsenal in the past. If a Gyokeres game, if we can call it that, requires the opposition to leave spaces he can use (as per Arteta’s quote above), then maybe this isn’t one of those.

However, on paper, and with all due respect to tomorrow’s opposition, if Hull at home isn’t a Gyokeres game, then it’s hard not to be a bit worried. Personally, I’d be all right to see him start this one, and if it doesn’t work then you have other options. Havertz is the obvious one, but Mikel Merino is an alternative as well with his ability to find spaces inside the opponent’s penalty area. Ideally though, it’s a bit of a building block of a game for the Swedish international. We need him to deliver goals and assists, the last few weeks have been a bit more positive in that regard, and ultimately it’s important that the manager recognises the moments in a season when he might have to choose one player over another for the overall benefit of the team.

Which is to say, I look forward very much to seeing Declan Rice starting up front against Hull tomorrow, and Arteta revealing that his tech-boffins have invented a gizmo which will allow Rice to player 180 minutes in every game. Plus injury time. I kid, obviously, but I suppose we should know by now that regardless of what we think about team selections, use of players, and everything else, this manager will never be even remotely swayed by external noise – and that’s a good thing most of the time!

We will talk a bit more about the Hull game in a quick preview podcast on Patreon a little later on. Because of the book launch, we’ll be recording before the manager meets the press, so bear that in mind.

Hopefully catch lots of you later on at Big Red – and here’s a nice piece on the photos in the book. Just a reminder again that those who have registered to come to the launch, you can purchase the book via the email sent to you (check your junk folders if you don’t see it), so you can simply come and collect on the night. We can also facilitate sales via credit card etc if you don’t want to do that.

If you purchased via the Patreon pre-sale, those books are not for collection tomorrow, and will be shipped to you in due course. There is also some scope to come along on spec on the night, and we should have copies available but obviously priority goes to those who registered. We will then have a final batch of books going on general sale online on Saturday evening after the Hull game. Full details of that to follow here on site and via Arseblog on BlueSky.

Best way to grind Research Points in Godzilla: Destroy All Monsters Melee Remastered

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Godzilla: Destroy All Monsters Melee Remastered has a fair number of things to unlock, and you’ll need plenty of Research Points. You get Research Points by just playing the game, but there’s an easy way to fast track the process.

Let’s find out the best way to grind Research Points in the remastered version.

The method I have mentioned there was present in the original version, but the grind was significantly easier. Remastered version has made things harder, but the core method remains the same.

Your first task is to unlock Kiryu from its research tree and pick the Survival mode. Then, pick Monster Island as the location and map to kickstart the process.

Shoot a rocket as soon as the match begins, and the AI will always block it. Then, get close to your enemy and grab them with the window that you have created. Once you grab the enemy, get close to the wall just behind the spot where the AI spawns.

You’ll find a lava pit at the location, and you can then use Kiryu’s fierce attack to launch the enemy over the wall without breaking it. Now, the enemy will take 10 damage per second due to the lava.

Once your main tasks is done, your next job is to keep them locked in the pit without failure. If they try to escape, grab and toss them in the lava once again.

When the enemy is in the pit, the wall will usually remain intact. However, the enemy can sometimes wall the hug and take no damage. If that happens, don’t break the wall yourself. Instead, bait them to do that task instead for you. Your only focus is to ensure that the enemy stays in the lava and keeps taking damage.

Airbnb is building an AI travel agent

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Airbnb is planning to launch an AI travel agent next year. The company shared details of the project during its Q3 2026 earnings call on Thursday, reiterating its intention to "build an AI-native Airbnb."

"AI is the best thing to ever happen to Airbnb. It's changing both how we develop products and how we operate," Airbnb said in a letter to shareholders. "AI is helping our teams move faster, and in Q3 alone, we shipped more than 50 products and features — nearly 70 percent more than a year ago."

Now Airbnb intends to add an AI travel agent to that collection. While still under development, the company says its new AI agent is being designed to help users research, plan, and book trips on its platform.

"We’re developing a custom-designed, AI-native experience that builds the interface around your question," said Airbnb. "Rather than a chat window, our agent draws on a library of hundreds of travel-specific interface elements — maps, photos, comparisons, calendars, itineraries — and composes them in real time to match what you’re looking for."

CEO Brian Chesky elaborated during Airbnb's earnings call, stating that the AI agent will "feel a little bit like generative AI" due to its ability to respond to users' requests with relevant custom interfaces for specific travel needs. Chesky further stated that the agent will have a "post-chat-based interface," being less text-focused, though it will still retain a "chat element."

"If you want a car rental, the agent generates this little UI of a car, and it's [presented] beautifully in a custom-generated interface," said Chesky. "If you want to invite co-travellers, the agent helps you."

Airbnb's AI agent will also help resolve issues once a booking has already been made, including changing reservations or assisting with refunds and rebookings. Interestingly, Airbnb states that the AI will provide 24-hour safety support as well, though did not elaborate on exactly what type of assistance it will offer.

"Our agent will help guests explore options, make decisions with the people they’re travelling with, and take action directly in Airbnb," Airbnb wrote. "Over time, we want our agent to help guests plan their entire trip in one place, across homes, hotels, services, experiences, and more."

This won't be restricted to bookings that are available on Airbnb either. Chesky stated that the AI agent will be able to go "outside the walls of Airbnb," though noted it will stay within the realm of travel.

"Today's AI agents, like [Meta's AI agent] Muse and [invite-only AI assistant] Instinct, are good at simple routine tasks like making a restaurant reservation or scheduling an appointment," Chesky said. "But planning a trip is different. Travel is aspirational. Discovering where to go and where to stay is part of the experience… I don't think the general-purpose agents are the best to do that. That is why we are building our own AI agent."

Airbnb's new AI travel agent is expected to be released in the first half of 2027.

This upcoming AI travel agent isn't to be confused with Airbnb's AI customer support assistant, which is already in operation. Airbnb states that its AI assistant resolved around 40 percent of all customer support issues without any human agents in Q3, an increase from approximately 30 percent last quarter. In Q2, Airbnb's AI agent resolved nearly 45 percent of issues that specifically began in the tool.

Airbnb reports that its revenue grew 19 percent to $4.9 billion in Q3, bringing in a net income of $1.9 billion.

Germany news: Trial of alleged 'Reichsbürger' group ringleaders begins

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Four members of the banned far-right "Kingdom of Germany" are on trial for belonging to a criminal organization. Meanwhile, a new study warns data centers could lead to water stress in Germany. Follow DW for more.

Read below for the top headlines from across Germany on Friday, November 6:

Four alleged ringleaders of the banned far-right "Kingdom of Germany" (KRD) are expected to appear before the Düsseldorf Higher Regional Court this morning.

The group is part of a conspiracy theorist movement that rejects the legitimacy of the modern German republic.

Authorities banned the KRD in May 2025 after monitoring its activities for more than a decade. Since then, the four defendants, including the group's so-called "King" Peter Fitzek, have been held in pre-trial detention.

They have been charged with membership in a criminal organization and other offenses, including document forgery and tax evasion amounting to millions of euros.

The Federal Prosecutor's Office accuses Fitzek and his co-defendants of seeking to replace the German republic with their own system. They are alleged to have set up "pseudo-state structures" such as a banking and insurance system, as well as a "registration office" that issued fictitious identity documents.

At the time the group was banned, hundreds of police officers carried out raids on 15 properties across Germany. They seized "further evidence of the association's anti-constitutional goals and activities," according to the Interior Ministry.

Authorities said the group had acquired land through donations with the aim of reducing the territory of Germany.

According to the prosecutor's office, they sought to expand their purported territory to the borders of the German Empire of 1871.

The group is estimated to have had around 1,000 followers at the time it was banned.

Guten morgen from the DW newsroom here on the banks of the Rhine in Bonn.

About 70 kilometers (43 miles) further north, the trial of four alleged ringleaders of the banned "Kingdom of Germany" (KRD) is expected to begin Friday in the city of Düsseldorf.

KRD is part of Germany's "Reichsbürger" movement, which rejects the modern German state and its structure as illegitimate. The group is accused of setting up its own structures, including a banking and insurance system.

Also making news, the German parliament is voting on whether to impose harsher penalties for the use of date rape drugs.

And, a new study suggests the expansion of data centers in Germany catering to the AI boom could lead to water stress.

This is Russell Martin’s last chance to change the narrative

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation plus sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Play the viral 442GOALS game and build an XI of Premier League legends!

The best features, fun and footballing quizzes, straight to your inbox every week.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Fantastic football content straight to your inbox! From the latest transfer news, quizzes, videos, features and interviews with the biggest names in the game, plus lots more.

Sign up to our FREE live football newsletter, tracking all of the biggest games available to watch on the device of your choice. Never miss a kick-off!

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

The Leicester City manager’s reputation may never recover if his side lose to non-League Oxford City tonight

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

As night falls this evening, the Leicester City team coach will edge through the rush-hour traffic on Marsh Lane and turn left through the unassuming entrance to Court Place Farm.

It will make its way along the narrow lane to the MGroup Stadium, where manager Russell Martin and his players will disembark with the sole intention of avoiding FA Cup humiliation against sixth-tier Oxford City.

When they board the coach again a few hours later, it will be with either relief or embarrassment, depending on whether they suffer a giant-killing that would make national and potentially international headlines. For Martin, it would be a defeat he may never be able to shake off.

That Leicester team coach has travelled to English football’s most famous stadiums, from Anfield to Wembley.

But its journey from the East Midlands to Oxfordshire for this FA Cup first round tie is a sign of how far the Foxes have fallen, just five years after lifting the trophy and only 10 years after winning the Premier League.

Martin’s managerial career - or perhaps more accurately, his reputation - has experienced a similarly sharp decline.

He was aged just 33 when he became MK Dons head coach in November 2019, when he was still on the club’s playing staff. In 2020/21, his only full season, the League One team boasted the third-highest possession percentage per game in Europe - behind only Manchester City and Barcelona - and scored a goal against Gillingham following a 56-pass move, a British record.

The best features, fun and footballing quizzes, straight to your inbox every week.

MK ended the campaign 13th, but Swansea City had seen enough to make Martin their manager. After solid but unspectacular finishes of 15th and 10th in the Championship, he moved to newly-relegated Southampton in summer 2023.

After a slow start, he led Saints to promotion back to the Premier League, via a club-record 25-game unbeaten run and a play-off final victory over Leeds United. It emerged this week that Martin was even earmarked as a future Manchester United manager by the club’s director of football, Jason Wilcox.

His stock had never been higher, but within six months it had crashed. Martin became a top-flight manager by favouring a possession-based style, but his refusal to change his approach became a stick to beat him with as Southampton floundered. He was sacked in December 2024 with Saints rock-bottom, having claimed just five points from 16 games.

Somehow, it got worse. Martin was named Rangers boss in summer 2025 but lasted just 123 days - the shortest stint by a permanent manager in the club’s history - after the fans turned on him amid poor results and some PR disasters.

It was why there was widespread derision when Leicester appointed the 40-year-old in June, as they prepared for life in League One following successive relegations. The jeers grew louder after some embarrassing early-season defeats, most notably a 4-0 thrashing by Oxford United at the King Power Stadium.

Fast forward to the present day and another clash with a team from Oxford, and Martin has won just 15 out of 53 games since leading Southampton to promotion. That being said, he is currently on his best run of form in that time, with Leicester taking 10 points from their past five matches.

The problem is that Martin has become such a lightning rod for criticism that whenever the Foxes drop points, the knives come out. Here are some of the comments under the club’s posts on X after the Foxes twice let slip a lead to draw 2-2 at home to Stevenage last Saturday:

“Get used to this with that clown as manager.”

“Whilst he remains in charge we remain in League One. His team selections, tactics, subs all questionable. As soon as we come up against anyone half decent we are found out. Get rid.”

“I want a flexible manager not a one-trick pony.”

“Your possession obsession is causing the team to go back rather than forward too much.”

While X is not always an accurate barometer of public opinion, these comments marked all the squares on the Russell Martin bingo card. To his critics, he is a "clown" and a "one-trick pony" ("fraud" is another popular insult), whose "possession obsession" means his teams are "found out" far too easily.

The latter criticism is certainly valid, as there is no doubt that his tactical inflexibility accelerated his downfall at Southampton and contributed to some of Leicester’s poor results this season.

Equally, the Venn diagram of people who do not like Martin and also hate ‘tippy tappy football’ probably has quite a crowded intersection. For some people, his failures vindicate their belief that a possession-based approach is overrated.

And if Leicester lose to Oxford City live on TV, it will vindicate their belief that Martin is a "clown".

Tonight’s game has all the ingredients of an FA Cup upset: an underperforming giant going to an in-form non-League club (City are second in National League North), who boast several former EFL players including ex-Sheffield Wednesday and Watford winger Fernando Forestieri and former Bristol City and Oxford United striker Matty Taylor.

City’s ground will be full to its 3,500 capacity, with the fans crammed in close to the action. The only thing missing is a playing surface resembling a mudbath, as City have a 3G pitch.

That should allow Leicester to dominate possession, but if they still come up short, well, Martin’s critics will feel they have been proved right.

Alternatively, if the Foxes win with little fuss, maybe the narrative around Martin will start to change. One victory over a non-League side will not move the needle, but you would expect a clown to slip on a banana skin, not calmly pick it up and toss it in the bin.

If Leicester then continue their good form and mount a promotion charge, he might even be praised for reversing his and the Foxes’ decline.

But that is a discussion for another day. Today, Martin must avoid a humiliating defeat from which his reputation may never recover.

James Roberts is a freelance sports journalist working for FourFourTwo and other titles. He started his career at the Oxford Mail, where he covered Oxford United home and away, before becoming a sports sub-editor for various national newspapers.

Lampard looks for Coventry progress as belief grows

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Coventry lost their opening four Premier League games by an aggregate of 10-0

"I would like to think there is a higher ceiling," says Frank Lampard.

After four opening defeats without scoring, a run which had Coventry written off, the Sky Blues are finding their feet.

They could move up to 11th in the Premier League with victory at Everton on Friday as Lampard returns to the Toffees, who he managed for a year from January 2022.

A win would leave them on 12 points, level with eighth-placed Bournemouth, a quarter of the way through their first season back in the Premier League in 25 years.

Just Middlesbrough (1996-97), Burnley (2009-10) and Cardiff (2013-14) have been relegated from the Premier League after taking 12 points from 10 games.

History is on Coventry's side as last season's Championship winners look to prove the doubters wrong.

Coventry fan impressed by Lampard's Premier League changes

Despite Coventry's start to the season - after defeats by Arsenal, Hull, Manchester City and Brighton - the optimism at the training ground did not waver.

The positive mood at the club's Ryton base remains, following Saturday's victory over Sunderland, as it did after the 5-0 home thumping by Brighton.

It was accepted the game, where Coventry played almost 45 minutes with 10 men following Taiwo Awoniyi's dismissal, can happen in the Premier League - and there was no hangover.

There was a belief there was enough evidence in performances against Hull, Manchester City and Arsenal the Sky Blues could compete in the top flight.

Then, after a 3-1 Carabao Cup defeat by Aston Villa, Lampard tweaked the system to match Nottingham Forest's back three to earn a 1-0 win at the City Ground.

"The result sometimes you can't quite control, but you can control your idea going into a game, that maximises our strengths and looks at the opposition's," said Lampard, who is in before 7.30am every morning, using the gym before training.

"There can be various reasons why we do it. We went to Nottingham Forest and we matched up their formation because I wasn't keen to go there in the moment we were in with a back four and the problems they can give you with wing-backs and 10s.

"It's not just my, or our, choice on formation which is going to win or lose games. We know we have a big fight so every small percentage in our set-up if we feel that way we will do it - but we must feel it's going to be the right one."

Lampard has always been tactically flexible and three wins in five games has allowed Coventry to climb out of the relegation zone.

The stats provide interesting reading too, especially when compared with the 1-0 defeat by Hull which could have been so damaging in the first home game of the season.

Then, Coventry had 68.5% possession but that dropped to 42.6% in the 3-1 win over Sunderland. The Sky Blues also made more than 200 fewer passes and more than 50 fewer forward passes against the Black Cats.

Their passes in the final third more than halved from 128 to just 60, as the patience - and even pragmatism - paid off as the Sky Blues begin to understand the demands of the division.

The benching of skipper Matt Grimes, key in last season's promotion, was also a big call from Lampard but one which has been met with understanding from within.

Coventry still have the lowest xG (expected goals) in the Premier League of 8.34 but lessons have been learned from the frustration of the opening weeks, even if those at the club recognise the difficult start given three of their opening four away games were at Manchester City, Arsenal and Tottenham.

After Everton, Coventry host Crystal Palace and Ipswich either side of a trip to Leeds to complete a run sources felt would be crucial to their survival hopes.

"When you're in the zone, enjoyment of the win is pretty short-lived - you switch your attention to the next game. Relaxation and complacency is the biggest danger in this game," said Lampard.

"If we can keep the strength of feeling, even when difficult results come, we will give ourselves a chance."

Jack Rudoni has attracted admirers after his start to the season

One Premier League manager has privately said if he were to take any players from Coventry, one of them would be Jack Rudoni.

The former Huddersfield midfielder - playing in League One four years ago - has been one of the quickest players to adapt for Coventry, with four goals in 10 appearances in all competitions, in a group short of top-flight experience.

Awoniyi (90 games) and Ethan Pinnock (117) have played the most with just Loum Tchaouna, Ellis Simms, Matt Grimes, Kaine Kesler-Hayden, Dan Bentley and Gus Hamer having featured in the Premier League.

Even then, Simms only made 12 appearances for Everton, Grimes just four for Swansea, Bentley 10 for Wolves and Kesler-Hayden had only played five minutes.

Tchaouna's and Hamer's experiences were relegation with Burnley and Sheffield United.

Lampard knew they would need time to find the grit to stay in the top flight but the Sky Blues have given themselves the chance.

"Sometimes you go through hundreds of games to find that resilience and a lot of our players haven't had that," added Lampard.

"There have been some complications. The squad and ourselves have managed pretty well when you see what we're trying to do."

La Gazzetta sul Milan: "Bocciato il turnover. Da Bartesaghi a Jashari: il Diavolo 2 non funziona"

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

In merito al Milan sconfitto ieri sera dal Ferencvaros, l'edizione odierna della Gazzetta dello Sport titola così: "Bocciato il turnover. Da Bartesaghi a Jashari: il Diavolo 2 non funziona". Ancora una volta le seconde linee non riescono ad incidere, ma va detto che la situazione non è migliorata più di tanto nemmeno dopo l'ingresso di alcuni titolarissimi. Senza i big i rossonero faticano da matti, serve quindi correre ai ripari a gennaio per rinforzare la rosa milanista che ad oggi non può competere per due comptizioni.

Nel post-partita, Ruben Amorim ha spiegato che secondo lui la sconfitta non è stata colpa del turnover: "Non si tratta della formazione titolare, si tratta della preparazione, di essere pronti mentalmente a vincere ogni partita e della responsabilità che abbiamo come club. Non importa chi scende in campo dal primo minuto, dobbiamo lottare per ogni seconda palla, curare i piccoli dettagli. Non parlo di tecnica, di fare magie o di vincere ogni singola gara: parlo delle piccole cose, di ciò che possiamo controllare. E oggi, specialmente nel primo tempo, non ci sono spiegazioni. Non ho nulla per difendere me stesso e i miei giocatori per la prestazione di oggi. La gestione dell'undici iniziale è legata anche al fatto che, se riproponiamo certi calciatori troppo spesso, rischiamo gli infortuni; devo gestire i minuti, devo immaginare come si svilupperà la partita, ma non voglio parlare della formazione titolare. Voglio dire che oggi non ci sono scuse per la nostra prestazione, soprattutto nei primi 45 minuti. Naturalmente lotteremo in questa competizione fino alla fine, ma la verità è che sembra che non riusciamo a mantenere lo stesso livello a distanza di tre giorni, ed è su questo che dobbiamo lavorare".

Apple’s new MacBook Pro just smashed our lab's display brightness records

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Join the Tom's Guide Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Unlock and manage exclusive Tom’s Guide member rewards.

Here at Tom’s Guide our expert editors are committed to bringing you the best news, reviews and guides to help you stay informed and ahead of the curve!

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Sign up to get the latest updates on all of your favorite content! From cutting-edge tech news and the hottest streaming buzz to unbeatable deals on the best products and in-depth reviews, we’ve got you covered.

Be AI savvy with your weekly newsletter summing up all the biggest AI news you need to know. Plus, analysis from our AI editor and tips on how to use the latest AI tools!

Unlock the vast world of Apple news straight to your inbox. With coverage on everything from exciting product launches to essential software updates, this is your go-to source for the latest updates on all the best Apple content.

Our weekly newsletter is expertly crafted to immerse you in the world of streaming. Stay updated on the latest releases and our top recommendations across your favorite streaming platforms.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The screen on the new MacBook Pro with PROLED XDR display is downright impressive. We measured close to 2,000 nits of brightness using a 10% HDR pattern. As someone who lab-tests every laptop that comes through Tom's Guide for review, I immediately wondered how Apple's latest display would stack up against the competition.

The numbers don't lie. After digging through our benchmark spreadsheets and comparing the results against 80 laptops tested in our lab this year, I can confidently say that Apple's latest MacBook Pro is the brightest laptop we've tested in 2026.

Everyone conducts display testing differently and uses different equipment, so results can vary by equipment and a publication’s testing standards. This is why it is important to understand how we test.

At Tom’s Guide, we test both a display's SDR and HDR performance. For SDR brightness testing, we display a white image on the screen, set the brightness to 100%, and measure it using our Klein K10-A and DisplayCal software.

For HDR testing, which is where we test a laptop’s maximum brightness, we use the same software and colorimeter, paired with copies of HDR test patterns. With the screen at 100% brightness, these patterns play as a movie in the laptop’s native movie player. We use 10%, 40%, and 100% HDR patterns. These patterns fill the screen with the corresponding percentages on a black background while playing.

And a lab tester’s note. The 10% window is a commonly used benchmark for evaluating peak HDR brightness, particularly in television testing. Smaller windows, such as 2% or 5%, can sometimes produce higher peak brightness readings, while larger windows help illustrate how a display handles brighter content across more of the screen.

In two of those HDR patterns (10% and 40%), the 16-inch MacBook Pro with the PROLED XDR display beat the list of 79 laptops that went through our lab this year. I am talking about both Mini LED and OLED displays. Now that’s impressive!

Sign up to the Tom's iGuide weekly newsletter with coverage on everything from product launches to software updates, this is your go-to source for the latest updates on all the best Apple content.

The 16-inch MacBook Pro with the PROLED XDR display is Apple’s current flagship, so it is our primary comparison point. On that unit, we measured 1,949 nits with a 10% pattern and 1,965 nits with a 40% pattern. This sets a new record for us in the lab.

Coming in second is the new Surface Laptop Ultra with RTX Spark. Equipped with a mini-LED display, it reaches 1,444 nits at 10% and 1,311 nits at 40%. In third place is the LG Gram 16, which is also an OLED laptop. It measured 1,067 nits at 10% and 1,033 nits at 40%.

As you can see, Apple's HDR results are nowhere close to what we measured on the Windows-powered OLED laptop. In fact, the new MacBook Pro is nearly twice as bright as LG's laptop.

Even more remarkable, Apple is outdoing itself. In our testing, the miniLED display on the Apple MacBook Pro 16-inch M5 Pro reached 1,427 nits on a 10% pattern and 1,444 nits on a 40% pattern. The new PROLED panel takes Apple's performance to an entirely new level.

What's also impressive is how consistently the new MacBook maintains its brightness. At 10%, we measured 1,949 nits, and increasing the illuminated area to 40% pushed that figure to 1,965 nits. That's a difference of just 16 nits. This shows Apple's new display isn't just producing impressive peak brightness in smaller HDR highlights; it's maintaining it across a much larger portion of the screen for a better HDR picture overall.

As someone who occasionally reviews TVs for Tom's Guide, I couldn't resist putting these numbers into another perspective. How does Apple's latest laptop compare with some of the brightest OLED TVs we've tested?

To be clear, this isn't an apples-to-apples comparison (pun intended). We test televisions in one of their most color-accurate picture modes, Filmmaker Mode, which can limit peak brightness. Meanwhile, our laptop brightness testing prioritizes maximum output. Nevertheless, it's an interesting comparison.

Currently, the brightest OLED TV we tested in Filmmaker Mode with a 10% pattern in HDR is the LG G6, which reaches 2,932 nits. Second among OLED TVs is the Samsung 65-inch S95H OLED TV, which reaches 2,721 nits. A budget OLED TV like the Samsung S90H, meanwhile, reaches 1,496 nits.

Samsung 65-inch S95H OLED TV (Filmmaker Mode)

Of course, other TV technologies are on the market. The Hisense UR9 currently holds the brightness record. Using Mini LED technology, it reached 4,370 nits — the brightest TV we tested to date.

Considering flagship OLED TVs cost nearly as much as a fully specced MacBook Pro, seeing Apple push nearly 2,000 nits on a portable laptop display says a lot about what you’re getting. It’s nearly TV-level quality.

Raw brightness is only half the story because image presentation and human perception matter just as much. Apple claims a 10,000,000:1 contrast ratio on these new OLED panels, and in person, it truly shows.

When lab testing at home, I had the Lenovo Yoga 9n with an OLED panel on the right, my personal mini LED MacBook Pro M3 Max on the left, and the new MacBook Pro with a PROLED XDR display in the middle. A 4K HDR nature video was playing as I peeked at the screens, and it was obvious which was the best.

On one screen showing a parrot, the difference was immediately noticeable. The new MacBook delivered particularly striking detail in the parrot’s individual feathers, and the greens looked more vibrant. Compared with the Mini-LED MacBook Pro and the Lenovo Yoga 9i, the image on Apple's new display looked considerably more lifelike.

As a final note, my colleague watched the “Dune: Part Three" trailer on the 16-inch MacBook Pro with the PROLED XDR display. While standing behind him as he took pictures, I paid close attention to skin tones and color rendition on the MacBook’s screen, as I do when reviewing a TV.

Having personally tested more than two dozen TVs this year, I can say the color reproduction was particularly impressive. In our lab tests, it covered 99.9% of the sRGB color gamut, which is why I came away with the impression of natural-looking skin tones and that exceptionally wide range of colors in the parrot video.

I was impressed by how closely the overall presentation reminded me of some of the best displays I've evaluated. Apple wasn’t playing around this year, and they have set the bar sky-high for the rest of the industry.

Follow Tom's Guide on Google News and add us as a preferred source to get our up-to-date news, analysis, and reviews in your feeds.

Arif is the New York-based lab tester at Future Media, Tom's Guide's parent company. He works closely with the head of testing Matthew Murray. Everyday, Arif runs rounds of benchmarking tests on laptops, phones, tablets, and tech gadgets for Future's editorial brands, including Tom's Guide and sister websites like Laptop Magazine. Arif has over eight years of experience in the technology journalism field and is known to be a collector of Windows PCs and laptops.

Godzilla Minus Zero ending explained: what happens to Ghidorah, who dies, and does it set up a sequel?

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Join the GamesRadar community for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Get the biggest gaming news, reviews, and releases straight to your inbox.

Bringing all the latest movie news, features, and reviews to your inbox

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Everything you need to know about the Godzilla Minus Zero ending explained

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The Godzilla Minus Zero ending is a dramatic one, with tons of kaiju action. So, you'd be forgiven on missing some of the finer details of what exactly goes down at the conclusion.

That's where we come in. Below, we've got a summary of the film's ending as well as a breakdown of all the biggest questions, so you can get up to speed at a glance. Naturally, that means there are spoilers ahead for the film. Turn back now if you're not up to date!

So, for everything you need to know about the Godzilla Minus Zero ending, head to the below. For even more on the film, check out our spoiler-free Godzilla Minus Zero review.

The Godzilla Minus Zero ending kicks off with a plan to bring Ghidorah and Godzilla to the same place in Ginza, Tokyo and have them fight. Ghidorah is lured to the location with generators, since the kaiju loves electricity, while Godzilla is lured there via a resonance wave machine made by biologist Kanji Murakami – the frequency attracts the kaiju when Noriko Shikishima is hooked up to it, since Noriko has Godzilla's DNA embedded in her after being attacked in the first film.

Godzilla and Ghidorah do indeed start fighting once they see each other, but Ghidorah is able to overpower Godzilla. The plan is to trap Ghidorah and freeze him with liquid nitrogen, but, when the trap is threatened by the destruction, Kenji Noda goes to fix the fuel lines himself – and his car is caught in a blast from Ghidorah. Noda is trapped, but Koichi Shikishima fires a missile from his plane to attack Ghidorah, accidentally killing Noda in the process. However, meteorologist Makoto Aoyama lies to him that Ghidorah was the one who killed Noda, sparing Koichi any further guilt.

Godzilla is then revived with the resonance machine, and both kaijus gear up for another showdown. This time, though, Godzilla is able to kill Ghidorah with his atomic ray. The US military then arrive on the scene and begin to attack Godzilla, before they are given orders to stand down.

Koichi holds Murakami at gunpoint, planning to escape with Noriko, before it's revealed that Murakami is on their side. Together, they destroy the plane and the resonance wave machine: Murakami has already set a trap to destroy his own research. This way, no one will be able to control Godzilla or use Noriko and her Godzilla DNA in the future, ending the cycle of war.

Godzilla returns to the ocean, but Murakami is captured by the US military before he can escape. Koichi and Noriko plan to go on the run and leave their child, Akiko, behind for her safety, but cannot bring themselves to abandon her. They end the film escaping together as a family.

Countless civilians die in Godzilla Minus Zero, but there is only one major character death. Hidetaka Yoshioka's Kenji Noda dies in the showdown with Ghidorah, when Ghidorah's energy blast crashes Noda's car and traps him. Koichi then fires a missile from his plane, which inadvertently kills Noda in the ensuing explosion – though, meteorologist Makoto Aoyama lies to him that Ghidorah was responsible for Noda's death to spare him further guilt.

No, New York City is not really attacked in Godzilla Minus Zero. This is a dream sequence from Min Tanaka's Kanji Murakami, who lost his family in the atomic bombing of Hiroshima. He secretly harbors a desire for revenge and wishes to unleash Godzilla on New York City to inflict the same kind of damage – though he is ultimately convinced against his plan by Kenji Noda.

Ghidorah takes a lot of damage in the film, but it's Godzilla who finally finishes him off. After Godzilla is seriously hurt by Ghidorah, he is able to regenerate and return for another round after Noriko and the team on the plane wake him with the resonance machine. He charges up his atomic breath and, this time, is finally able to kill Ghidorah.

After winning his battle with Ghidorah, Godzilla simply returns to the sea. It seems the US military had designs on controlling the kaiju with the resonance wave machine, though luckily it gets destroyed before anyone can get their hands on it. That means Godzilla is free – though that probably means he's likely to continue his rampages, which could be bad news for Tokyo.

This is a resonance wave machine, which is capable of attracting Godzilla thanks to the kaiju's DNA Noriko still carries in her body after being attacked in the previous film. The machine is used to lure Godzilla to the site of the showdown with Ghidorah, though, when the US military arrives, everyone onboard the ship makes the decision to destroy the machine and everything in the plane so it cannot be used to control Godzilla's movements in the future and continue the cycle of war. All of Kanji Murakami's research is also destroyed.

The US military wants to use the resonance wave machine so that they can control Godzilla's movements (though, this wouldn't control Godzilla entirely, it would just make it possible to lure him somewhere). It's heavily implied that the US desires Godzilla and his atomic breath as a weapon when troops are ordered not to hurt the kaiju.

No, Koichi and Noriko do not leave Akiko behind. In the end, they don't have the heart to after Akiko gets extremely upset at the idea of them abandoning her. Of course, the young couple think they're doing the right thing, since they're likely to be on the run forever thanks to Noriko's Godzilla DNA and Koichi's role in helping to destroy the resonance wave machine. Still, Akiko is probably better off with her parents than without them, so it's a welcome sight to see the youngster on the motorbike with Koichi and Noriko at the end.

Godzilla Minus Zero certainly leaves the door open for a third film, though nothing has been confirmed yet. Koichi and Noriko are on the run with Akiko, and Godzilla escaped into the ocean, while Kanji Murakami is in the custody of the US military. These are all plot threads that could be picked up in a third film.

"I have been a huge Godzilla fan since I was a small child, and I swung for the fences with Godzilla Minus Zero," director Takashi Yamazaki recently told SFX magazine. "[These films] are huge milestones, looking back at my career and in my life so far. But that doesn't mean I would never work on another Godzilla film again. If Toho came to me with another offer to explore this world even further, I'd probably say yes.

"Yet at the same time, if you told me this is my last Godzilla film, I would be quite satisfied with what we were able to achieve," he continued. "I'm very grateful to Toho for trusting me with their baby. I'm just happy that I was a Godzilla fan, because that's what enabled me to do this." So, we'll just have to wait and see.

Godzilla Minus Zero is in theaters now. For more, check out our guide to all the most exciting upcoming movies left of 2026.

The lowdown on the Godzilla Minus Zero credits

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Does Godzilla Minus Zero have a post-credits scene? It's a fair question, since a lot of movies are hiding bonus material in stingers at the end of the movie.

Below, we've got everything you need to know about whether Godzilla Minus Zero has a post-credits scene or not, including a spoiler-free section on what you're waiting for, and then a breakdown that reveals just what is lurking at the end of the credits.

So, for everything you need to know, head to the below. For even more, check out our Godzilla Minus Zero review.

There is technically no Godzilla Minus Zero post-credits scene, however there is an audio tease at the very end of the credits. So, if you absolutely don't want to miss a thing, remain seated until the credits have finished rolling.

The Godzilla Minus Zero post-credits scene isn't exactly a scene per se, but rather a tease. As the credits come to an end, we hear the earth-shattering sound of Godzilla stomping, as well as his almighty roar. It's a reminder that the fearsome kaiju is still out there, even though he vanished into the sea at the end of the film.

It's unclear if a third film will happen, but this certainly leaves the door open for more. "I have been a huge Godzilla fan since I was a small child, and I swung for the fences with Godzilla Minus Zero," director Takashi Yamazaki recently told SFX magazine. "[These films] are huge milestones, looking back at my career and in my life so far. But that doesn't mean I would never work on another Godzilla film again. If Toho came to me with another offer to explore this world even further, I'd probably say yes.

"Yet at the same time, if you told me this is my last Godzilla film, I would be quite satisfied with what we were able to achieve," he added. "I'm very grateful to Toho for trusting me with their baby. I'm just happy that I was a Godzilla fan, because that's what enabled me to do this."

Bergomi: "Mi hanno detto che il Como doveva fermarsi col Lens, ho risposto così"

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Bergomi: "Mi hanno detto che il Como doveva fermarsi col Lens, ho risposto così"

Installa la nostra App gratuita e accedi più velocemente alle notizie

Remove Duplicate Lines From a 48,000-Line CSV Without Losing the Original Order

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 6 • Originally published at meng4099244-ship-it.github.io

Last month I had to merge two exports from our billing system into one import file. The first export had 31,207 customer email addresses, the second had 17,706, and a few thousand lines overlapped. The CRM import endpoint rejects the whole file if it contains a single repeated email, so deduplication had to happen before upload.

There was a second constraint that tripped me up: row order drove import priority. Whichever line appeared first had to stay. So sort -u, the command half of my brain reached for, was off the table from the start. Here are three approaches that do work, tested on Ubuntu 22.04 with Python 3.11.1 and GNU awk 5.1.1.

The obvious one-liner is list(set(lines)). I've written it myself under deadline pressure. Don't. A set iterates in arbitrary hash order, so the output is a shuffled file, and the CRM import would run in whatever order the hash table happened to land on.

What you want is a seen set plus a counter:

Reading line by line means the file never sits in memory as one blob. The seen set held about 45,000 short strings, roughly 6 MB on my machine. I've pushed a 2 GB access log through the same shape of script by printing to stdout and redirecting; it took a few minutes but finished. Membership checks against a set are O(1) on average, which is why this stays fast as the file grows. The same pattern exists in JavaScript as a Set, so porting the script is a ten-minute job if your pipeline is Node-based.

When I just need the result and don't want to write a file, GNU awk does it in a single command:

$0 is the entire line. The expression seen[$0]++ returns 0 the first time a line shows up (falsy, so awk prints it) and counts upward after that. First occurrence wins, original order preserved. Same semantics as the Python script. The gawk manual has a whole chapter on how arrays and their uninitialized elements behave, worth a read if this idiom feels too magical.

One honest caveat: awk accumulates every line it has seen in memory, same as the Python set. For a 40 GB file on a 4 GB box neither will end well, and you'd reach for sort with a stable flag or a disk-based approach. For anything under a few hundred megabytes it's fine.

Sometimes the input arrives through Slack as a pasted list of a few thousand lines, from a teammate on Windows who has no intention of opening a terminal. For those, I use a free online duplicate line remover. Paste, click, copy the result back out.

This browser-based tool deletes duplicate lines while keeping the first occurrence and the original order, which is the same semantics as the two methods above. The processing happens client-side in the page, so the paste never leaves the machine. With customer email addresses involved, that's the part I actually care about, and it's faster than explaining to a colleague how to run awk in PowerShell.

The second export came out of a Windows service. Every line ended in \r\n, and my rstrip("\n") left the carriage return in place. On screen the lines looked identical; in memory they weren't:

First run reported dropped 3203. After I changed the strip to line.rstrip("\r\n"), the same file reported dropped 3847. Those extra 644 "duplicates" were the same addresses, invisible until the repr showed the \r.

The export was saved as "UTF-8 with BOM", which many spreadsheet tools do by default. The very first line carries a three-byte prefix that repr makes easy to spot:

'\ufeffalice@example.com' never equals 'alice@example.com', so if that address appears again later, it survives as a duplicate. Opening the file with encoding="utf-8-sig" strips the BOM on read and the problem disappears. I now check the first byte of any vendor CSV before writing the script.

A teammate "fixed" one of these files with sort -u in a cron job. Dedup worked. The import priority did not, because the output came back sorted, not in original order. Locale changes the collateral damage, too:

Same input, same machine, two different outputs depending on LC_ALL. The GNU coreutils sort documentation covers the field and locale behavior in detail. When order matters, dedupe with awk or the streaming script instead, and save sort -u for cases where sorted output is what you wanted anyway.

For files in the gigabytes I use the Python script. For quick shell work, awk. For a pasted list from a colleague, the browser tool. All three preserve the first occurrence, and that's usually the only thing the downstream system actually checks.

More free browser-based utilities at utilvy.com.

Reading a 2 km laser rangefinder module over UART with Python (LR2000E2, 55 AA protocol)

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A laser rangefinder module is one of the easiest long-range sensors to wire up and one of the easiest to misread. The wiring is four wires. The mistakes happen in the protocol: a frame header you skip, a checksum you do not verify, a unit you assume. This post walks through a small, complete Python reader for the ERDI LR2000E2, a 905 nm UART laser rangefinder module with a published range of 4 to 2,000 m, and shows where the traps are.

Everything below comes from the LR2000E2 user manual (v1.2, public edition 2026-09-06) and the product page at https://erdilrf.com/models/lr2000e2/. Data checked on 2026-11-06.

Two numbers matter for software. The rate is adaptive, so you should not build a 10 Hz control loop around it; treat it as a slow, long-reach sensor. And the "valid measurement rate" in the manual is 98 % or better up to 400 m and 80 % or better from 400 to 2,000 m, which means at long range one reading in five can legitimately be a failure. Your code has to handle failed readings as a normal case, not an exception.

Two practical notes. First, the manual states the 3.3–5 V supply range but does not separately specify the logic level of TXD and RXD. If you power the module at 5 V and talk to a 3.3 V-only microcontroller pin, measure TXD with a scope or meter first, or ask the manufacturer for the model-specific statement. A USB-serial adapter that supports 3.3 V levels is the safe starting point. Second, pin 6 is an enable input: the module expects it high to operate, so tie it according to the manual before wondering why nothing answers.

The inrush current is below 400 mA, so use a supply that can deliver it without sagging. A weak USB hub that browns out at power-on will look like a protocol problem.

Frames are 8 bytes. A command is 55 AA D1 D2 D3 D4 , where the checksum is the low byte of the sum of the function code and the four data bytes. The reply is also 8 bytes: 55 AA STA FF DIS_H DIS_L . STA = 1 means success; any other value means the measurement failed. The distance is DIS_H * 256 + DIS_L, and the manual states that all results are output as the actual value × 10, so you divide by 10 to get metres.

You can check the checksum column by hand: 0x88 + 4 × 0xFF = 0x484, low byte 0x84.

sync() instead of ser.read(8). Reading a fixed 8 bytes works until the first dropped byte, after which every frame is misaligned and you decode garbage forever. Searching for 55 AA first costs nothing and removes that failure class. Note the small gap: a data byte pair of 55 AA inside a payload would false-trigger, which is why the strict checksum option exists.

strict defaults to off. The manual text describes the reply checksum as the sum from the first header byte to DATA4. Enable strict=True on your bench first and log what you see; if your unit's replies agree with the formula, leave it on in production. If they do not, you have found a document or firmware difference worth reporting to the supplier before you design anything around it.

Failed readings are normal. ok=False is returned for a bad frame and for STA != 1. Keep the last good value with a timestamp and a staleness limit instead of passing None downstream.

Units. Dividing by 10 is specific to this model family. Other modules from other vendors, and even other ERDI families, report millimetres or centimetres. Never copy a divisor from a sibling model; take it from that model's manual.

You do not need a 2 km line of sight to test the software. Point the module at a wall at a known distance such as 5 m or 20 m, measure with a tape, and log 100 single shots. Plot the histogram. You are checking three things: the mean against the tape, the spread against the ±1 m accuracy band, and the fraction of STA != 1 readings. For targets that are well inside the range, the failure fraction should be very small. If it is not, check supply sag and the SW-SHOT line before suspecting the optics.

For the long-range claim, treat the datasheet range as a statement about a target and conditions, not a promise. The accuracy table does not specify target reflectivity and size, and real reach depends on weather, target colour, angle and sun. A low-reflectivity target at an oblique angle will fall short of 2,000 m. Plan for margin.

If you would rather not maintain this yourself, ERDI publishes an open-source driver set for its 905 nm modules (Python, Arduino and ROS 2) at https://github.com/ErdiTech/erdi-lrf-drivers. Each constant in it is cited to the model's manual, which is the point: the unit divisor, baud rate and frame length are per model, not per family.

What is the maximum range of the LR2000E2? The manufacturer's table gives 4 to 2,000 m as the standard range, with accuracy of ±1 m up to 400 m and 0.4 % of distance beyond. Actual reach depends on target reflectivity, size and weather.

What baud rate does it use? 115200 bps, 8 data bits, frame length 8 bytes.

How do I convert the reading to metres? Combine the two data bytes into an integer and divide by 10.

Can I power it from 5 V? The supply range is 3.3 to 5 V. The signal-pin logic level is not stated separately in the manual, so verify before connecting to a 3.3 V-only input.

Is it eye-safe? The manufacturer states Class 1. Check the model's own document and local regulations for your product.

What does it cost? USD 120 per unit for 1 to 30 pieces, with shipping included, as published on the product page on 2026-11-06.

Salesforce vs HubSpot vs Pipedrive for Small Teams: How the Three Actually Differ

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 6 • Originally published at saasstatshub.com

The three entry plans are different shapes of product, and that — not the per-seat price — is usually the deciding factor. Pipedrive Lite ($14/seat/month annual) is a focused sales-pipeline tool. HubSpot Starter ($7/seat/month annual offer price for new customers, $20 normal) is a five-hub starter bundle that adds starter-level marketing, service, and content tools to the CRM. Salesforce Starter Suite ($25/user/month) is a simplified all-in-one CRM whose main draw is the upgrade path into the Salesforce ecosystem.

One feature splits them sharply: email sync with your inbox is included at HubSpot Starter, starts at Pipedrive Growth ($39), and is not confirmed for Salesforce Starter Suite on its official pages.

Read this as "what the official pages state," not as an exhaustive feature audit. The email-sync row is the one most likely to eliminate a vendor outright — if your team expects email conversations inside the CRM, Pipedrive's entry price for that need is the Growth plan, not Lite.

Qualifiers that travel with these numbers: HubSpot's $7/$10 are offer prices with renewal terms not stated; Salesforce labels its prices "starting price" and marks its page "subject to change"; Pipedrive's monthly rate was not verifiable. Put your must-have feature list next to the numbers before comparing.

The full comparison, with sources and the pricing checklist, is on SaaSStatsHub: https://saasstatshub.com/crm/salesforce-vs-hubspot-vs-pipedrive/

Wabi Not for You? Pick the Right Alternative by What You Actually Need

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

I'm the author of Wabi Wiki, an unofficial guide to the Wabi AI assistant — not affiliated with Wabi or Replika. This post is a rewritten version of our alternatives guide, written for Dev.to.

Wabi is a multiplayer personal AI assistant that builds mini-apps inside your chats — genuinely different from anything else out there. But different isn't the same as right for you. It launched publicly on October 19, 2026, it's free and English-first, and it runs on iPhone, Android and Mac only. If any of that rules it out for you, here's the honest map of where to look instead, sorted by the need Wabi doesn't meet.

As of October 2026 there is no Windows app and no web version of Wabi, and none has been announced. If your day happens on a Windows PC, the practical answer today is a mainstream assistant in the browser — ChatGPT, Claude or Gemini. You lose the multiplayer threads and the auto-built mini-apps, which are the whole reason Wabi is interesting. You gain an assistant that actually runs on the machine in front of you. If those group mini-apps are the point, run Wabi on your phone and treat the PC as a spectator for now.

Wabi is built to get things done, not to be your friend. If what you actually want is one-on-one conversation and emotional companionship, look at AI companion apps — Replika first, since it comes from Wabi's own founder. Expect freemium: the chat is free, the deeper relationship features sit behind a subscription. Our Wabi Wiki comparison of the two lays out which job each one is actually built for.

Wabi is days old and its maker openly says the business model isn't decided yet. Fun for a side experiment; risky as the backbone of a critical workflow. If AI is load-bearing in your job today, pick an established assistant or productivity tool with a paid plan and a track record as your primary — and let Wabi earn its way in over time.

Sometimes the honest answer is that you don't need an AI at all. A shared calendar, a shared list and a group chat handle coordination without connecting anyone's bank account to anything. Wabi's edge is doing the legwork for the group. If you don't need the legwork done, the simple tools win on trust and cost.

Still leaning Wabi? Read the full alternatives guide on Wabi Wiki, then follow the safe download steps — and if privacy is what's holding you back, read the safety facts first.
